# CranioZ Glossary

**Document type:** Reference
**Status:** Living document
**Scope:** Canonical definitions of terms used across the CranioZ documentation

---

## 1. Purpose

This glossary provides a single, authoritative definition for each term used in the CranioZ architecture and design documentation.

It exists to:

- Eliminate ambiguity when the same term appears in multiple documents.
- Provide a canonical reference for contributors and reviewers.
- Distinguish terms that are easily confused (e.g., *Area* vs. *Editor*, *Tool* vs. *Command*, *Datatype* vs. *Semantic Type*).
- Link each term to the document(s) that define its detailed contract.

**Rule:** when a document uses a term defined here, it should use it with the meaning given here. If a document needs a different meaning, it must declare that explicitly and link back to this glossary.

---

## 2. How to Use This Glossary

- **Definitions here are normative.** A document may expand a definition, but it must not contradict it.
- **Terms are grouped by domain** (Architecture, UI, Domain, Application, etc.) for navigability.
- **Cross-references** point to the document(s) that specify the term in detail.
- **Aliases and deprecated terms** are listed explicitly to prevent their reintroduction.

If a term is missing, add it here first, then reference it from the relevant document.

---

## 3. Architectural Terms

### ADR — Architecture Decision Record
A document that records a significant architectural decision, its context, alternatives considered, and consequences. CranioZ ADRs live under `docs/adr/` and follow a numbered convention (`ADR-0001`, `ADR-0002`, ...).

**See:** `docs/adr/`

### Application Layer
The layer that coordinates system operations: Commands, Services, Tasks, Flows, Events, transactions, and application-level state. It orchestrates the Domain and Infrastructure layers.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §5.2

### Capability
An operational capability that can be applied to an Object, resolved by the system from the Object's Datatype, Semantic Type, Components, and context. Capabilities are not statically stored on the Object.

**Distinguish from:** *Tool* (a user-facing interaction), *Component* (data/structure the Object has).

**See:** `docs/architecture/objects/objects.md` §5

### Command
An executable application operation. Every persistent state-changing operation in CranioZ passes through the Command mechanism, which provides validation, transaction boundaries, undo/redo, event publication, and auditability.

**Distinguish from:** *Tool* (an interaction interface that may produce a Command), *Service* (reusable functionality).

**See:** `docs/architecture/commands/commands.md`

### Component
Additional information or structure associated with an Object (e.g., `Transform`, `LandmarkSet`, `AnatomicalProperties`). A Component answers "What does the Object have?"

**Distinguish from:** *Capability* ("What can be done with the Object?").

**See:** `docs/architecture/objects/objects.md` §4

### Datatype
The fundamental structure of the data manipulated by CranioZ (e.g., `Mesh`, `Volume`, `Image2D`, `Curve`, `Point`, `PointCloud`, `ROI`). A Datatype does not, by itself, define clinical meaning.

**Distinguish from:** *Semantic Type* (what the data represents).

**See:** `docs/architecture/objects/datatypes.md`

### Domain Layer
The layer that contains the conceptual model: Patient, Project, Scene, Object, Datatype, Semantic Type, Component, Capability, and clinical rules. It is independent of Qt, VTK, filesystem, and any infrastructure concern.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §5.1`

### Flow
A versioned, declarative sequence of steps that guides a user through a clinical task by coordinating steps, requirements, and modules. Flow definitions are stored as JSON; the execution engine belongs to the Application layer.

**See:** `docs/architecture/clinical_workflows/clinical_workflows.md`

### Infrastructure Layer
The layer that contains technical implementations: DICOM access, VTK/ITK integration, file I/O, persistence, and external library adapters. It implements the ports defined by the Application layer.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §5.3`

### Module
A coherent functional capability that composes Tools, Commands, Services, Data Types, Editors, and Flows. Modules declare their UI composition via a `ModuleUISpec`; they do not own Areas or Editors.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §6, `docs/architecture/modules/modules.md`

### Object
The entity manipulated by CranioZ, understood through four dimensions: Data (Datatype), Semantic (Semantic Type), Components, and Capabilities.

**See:** `docs/architecture/objects/objects.md`

### Plugin
An external extension that provides Modules, Tools, Services, Data Types, UI components, Flows, or Capabilities through public extension points. Plugins must use versioned public APIs, not internal implementation details.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §17`

### Ports and Adapters
The architectural pattern used by CranioZ. The Application layer defines ports (interfaces) for persistence, project loading/saving, external computation, and rendering integration. Infrastructure provides concrete adapters.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §16`

### Scene
The domain-level spatial organization of objects within a specific planning context. The Scene defines object membership, spatial hierarchy, transforms, reference frames, and the coordinate system. It does not own the lifecycle of domain objects.

**Distinguish from:** *DomainObjectStore* (owns canonical object identity and lifecycle), *Rendering Representation* (how it is visualized).

**See:** `docs/architecture/scene/scene.md`

### Semantic Type
The clinical meaning assigned to an Object (e.g., `Anatomy.Bone.Mandible`, `Imaging.CT`, `Surgical.Guide`). The same Datatype (e.g., `Mesh`) can carry different Semantic Types.

**Distinguish from:** *Datatype* (the structure of the data).

**See:** `docs/architecture/objects/datatypes.md`, `docs/architecture/objects/objects.md` §3

### Service
Reusable application or domain functionality not naturally belonging to a single entity. Services have explicit contracts.

**Distinguish from:** *Command* (a specific executable operation), *Tool* (a user-facing interaction).

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §9`

### Task
A potentially long-running operation managed by the Application layer, providing execution, progress, cancellation, error propagation, and result delivery.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §9`

### Tool
An operational capability exposed to the user. A Tool defines what it represents, how it is identified, in which contexts it is available, and how interaction is initiated. Execution is delegated to Commands and Application Services.

**Distinguish from:** *Command* (the executable operation), *Widget* (a reusable UI component).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/tools/tools.md`

---

## 4. UI Terms

### Area
A structural container provided by the Workspace. It occupies a position in the Area Layout Tree and hosts one or more Editors, organized in its own internal Editor Layout Tree. There is no `EditorHost` — the Area manages its Editors directly.

**Distinguish from:** *Editor* (functional unit), *Region* (a geometric grouping of Areas), *Overlay* (a floating layer).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §2, ADR-0001

### Area Layout Tree
The N-ary split tree that organizes the Areas of the Workspace. Nodes are `Leaf` (one Area) or `Split` (direction + two or more children). The Area Layout Tree is the single source of truth for Workspace structure.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §5`

### Editor
A functional unit hosted inside an Area. It provides a specific interaction experience (e.g., `Viewport3DEditor`, `MPRViewerEditor`, `ConsoleEditor`). An Editor declares and materializes its own Toolbar(s) and internal overlays.

**Distinguish from:** *Area* (the structural container), *Tool* (an action), *Toolbar* (the presentation of Tools inside an Editor).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md`, ADR-0001, ADR-0002

### Editor Layout Tree
The N-ary split tree internal to an Area that organizes the Editors hosted by that Area. It uses the same node types as the Area Layout Tree (`Leaf`, `Split`).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §5`, `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §8

### EditorCatalog
The context-aware filter over the `EditorRegistry`. It answers: "Which registered Editors can be used in the current context?" It considers the active Scene, Project, Area, and required capabilities/data.

**Distinguish from:** *EditorRegistry* (the registry of all Editor types).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §6

### EditorContext
The controlled set of dependencies an Editor receives at insertion time. Created once per Editor (hybrid model): fields specific to the Editor (identity, view state, extras) are isolated; shared services (SceneQuery, CommandDispatcher, EventSubscription, EditorServices) are propagated by reference.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §15, ADR-0002

### EditorRegistry
The registry of all Editor **types** known to the system. It resolves `editor_id` to concrete Editor classes and creates instances. It does **not** manage instances.

**Error contracts:** `register` raises `ValueError` on duplicate; `resolve` and `create` raise `KeyError` on unknown.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §5, `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §3.6

### LayoutBuilder
The component that consumes a `ModuleUISpec` and produces a `LayoutTree`. It is a **pure** composition step: it does not import PySide6, does not require a `QApplication`, and does not create widgets.

**Distinguish from:** *LayoutRenderer* (materializes widgets).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §3.5`, ADR-0002

### LayoutRenderer
The component that materializes a `LayoutTree` as Qt widgets (nested `QSplitter` widgets and Area widgets). It is the sole materializer of widgets: Areas, Splits, Editors, and Toolbars.

**Distinguish from:** *LayoutBuilder* (pure composition step).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/implementation/workspace-implementation.md` Phase 6, ADR-0002

### LayoutState
The presentation state associated with a Workspace layout: proportions (`QSplitter` sizes per node), visibility (which Areas are shown or hidden), and the active Editor per Area. Stored separately from the structural trees.

**Distinguish from:** *OverlayState* (state of Overlays), *ViewStateStore* (per-Editor presentation state).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §5.3

### MainWindow
The top-level application window. It hosts the Workspace and provides the application frame (native OS window controls and, when applicable, the OS menu bar). It does not own the Workspace layout state, clinical state, or workflow semantics.

**See:** `architecture/main_window/main_window.md`

### ModuleUISpec
The declarative specification a Module provides to the Workspace, describing which Areas it wants the Workspace to materialize, their placement, and their internal Editor Layout Trees.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §3.5

### Overlay
A floating layer managed by the Workspace, positioned over a Region of Areas. Overlays can be moved, resized, and closed. The `steps_panel` is the primary example.

**Distinguish from:** *Top Bar* (structural component, not repositionable), *Editor internal overlay* (belongs to an Editor, not the Workspace).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §7`

### OverlayState
The presence, configuration, and anchoring of Overlays. Stored alongside the layout trees, inside the project file.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §5.3`, §7.6

### Placement
A semantic classification of an Area within the Workspace layout: `LEFT`, `CENTRAL`, `RIGHT`, `BOTTOM`. Placement is used to build the initial Area Layout Tree and to associate Areas with Regions. It is preserved after construction.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §3.5`

### Region
A geometric area derived from the Area Layout Tree that groups related Areas. Overlays are positioned over Regions.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §7.2`

### Surface
A visual background belonging to a window, panel, or overlay. CranioZ defines three surface styles: **Solid**, **Translucent**, and **Frosted Glass**.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/surfaces-and-overlays.md`

### Theme
The global visual palette and styling configuration of the interface (Light, Dark, System). A theme styles interface components; it does not dictate the background of every viewport.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/themes-and-design-tokens.md`

### Toolbar
A widget internal to an Editor that presents Tools to the user. A Toolbar is declared by a `ToolbarSpec` and materialized by the `LayoutRenderer` from the Editor's `toolbar_specs()`. The Workspace never hosts Toolbars; the Area never hosts Toolbars.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/toolbar/toolbar.md`, ADR-0002

### Top Bar
A structural component of the Workspace, fixed at the top, not repositionable or closeable. It contains three segments: Left (menu, Home, toggle Left), Center (module tabs, `+` button), Right (Extras, toggle Right, window controls).

**Distinguish from:** *Overlay* (repositionable, closeable).

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §3.3`

### Widget
A reusable interface component implementing the CranioZ visual and interaction conventions. Generic composite widgets live in `src/cranioz/ui/widgets/`. Editor-internal overlays live in `src/cranioz/ui/editors/overlays/`.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/widget-system.md`, ADR-0003

### Workspace
The runtime environment where clinical work happens. It is a composition of Areas (structural containers that host Editors) and Overlays (floating layers). The Workspace manages Areas and Overlays; it does not own clinical state, Tools, Toolbars, or Module behavior.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md`

---

## 5. Domain Terms

### Clinical Flow
See *Flow*.

### DomainObjectStore
The canonical registry and lifecycle authority for domain objects belonging to a Project. It is Project-scoped, not application-global.

**Distinguish from:** *Scene* (spatial organization of objects).

**See:** `docs/architecture/scene/scene.md` §4

### Patient
The person associated with clinical data in CranioZ. The Patient is composed of nine section value objects (identity, documents, filiation, address, complaint, comorbidities, medications, antecedents, habits). It is referenced by a Project; it does not own clinical data such as imaging, meshes, photographs, odontogram, or documents.

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/patient/patient.md`, ADR-0004

### PatientSummary
A lightweight read-only projection of the Patient carrying `patient_id`, `display_name`, `birth_date`, and `sex`. Used in contexts where the full record is not needed (Project Catalog, ProjectContext, tooltips).

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/patient/patient.md` §13

### Project
The persistent clinical container associated with a patient and one or more clinical workflows. It provides the boundary within which patient-related data, imaging, models, planning data, workflow state, metadata, and other clinical artifacts are organized.

**Distinguish from:** *Patient* (the person referenced by the Project), *ProjectContext* (the runtime representation of an opened Project).

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/project/project.md`

### Project Catalog
A read-only discovery projection of available projects. It allows the Home Page to efficiently display and search existing projects without loading complete clinical data.

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/project/project.md` §5`

### Project Manager
The single application-level authority for the lifecycle of a project: creation, opening, updating, closing, deletion, archival, restoration, validation, access checks, persistence coordination, and lifecycle events.

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/project/project.md` §4`

### Project Repository
The persistence abstraction through which the Project Manager loads and stores project state. It does not decide whether a project should be created, updated, opened, or deleted.

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/project/project.md` §12`

### Project Storage
The infrastructure responsible for the physical representation of a project (filesystem, database, institutional storage, remote storage, etc.).

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/project/project.md` §11`

### ProjectContext
A runtime initialization snapshot created when a project is opened. It is immutable for the Workspace, contains no physical paths, and does not own the project lifecycle.

**See:** `docs/architecture/domain/project/project.md` §17`

### Scene (Domain)
See *Scene* in §3.

### Workflow
See *Flow*.

---

## 6. Application and Command Terms

### CommandBus
The mechanism through which Editors and Tools publish Commands. The CommandBus routes Commands to the appropriate CommandHandler.

**See:** `docs/architecture/commands/commands.md`

### CommandDispatcher
The interface through which an Editor or Tool dispatches Commands. Part of the `EditorContext` and `ToolContext`.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §15

### CommandHandler / CommandExecutor
The component responsible for validating a Command, opening a transaction, applying changes to the Domain/Scene, committing, and publishing events.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/workspace/workspace.md` §3.4`

### Event Bus
The mechanism through which the Application layer publishes events after a successful commit. Events communicate state changes; they do not replace Commands.

**See:** `docs/architecture/overview.md` §13`

### SelectionService
The service through which an Editor or Tool queries and observes the current selection. Part of the `EditorContext` and `ToolContext`.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §15

### SceneQuery
The read-only interface through which an Editor accesses the Scene. An Editor never receives a mutable reference to the Scene.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §15

### ToolContext
A strict subset of the `EditorContext` provided to a Tool. Contains `SceneQuery`, `SelectionService`, `CommandDispatcher`, and `ToolServices`. Does not contain `EventSubscription`, `ViewStateStore`, or `EditorServices`.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §15

### ViewStateStore
Per-Editor presentation state (zoom, camera, current slice, visual selection, presentation mode). It is not clinical state and not the same as `LayoutState`.

**See:** `docs/architecture/ui/editors/editors.md` §15`

---

## 7. Design System Terms

### Design Token
A named value used by the design system, representing a semantic role rather than a hard-coded component-specific value. Categories include color, typography, spacing, shape, and elevation.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/themes-and-design-tokens.md`

### Frosted Glass
A translucent surface combined with blur of the content behind it. Optional presentation capability, not a requirement.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/surfaces-and-overlays.md`

### Modal Focus Effect
A presentation effect that visually emphasizes an active modal dialog by reducing the visual prominence of the Workspace behind it (dimming, optionally with blur).

**Status:** Blur behind VTK renderers is experimental; dimming-only is the initial default.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/modal-focus-effect.md`

### Solid Surface
An opaque surface with a theme-derived background color. Default for persistent panels, clinical forms, and information-dense dialogs.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/surfaces-and-overlays.md`

### Translucent Surface
A surface with partial opacity through which the underlying content remains visible. Suitable for selected floating toolbars and compact contextual controls.

**See:** `docs/architecture/appearance/surfaces-and-overlays.md`

---

## 8. Internationalization Terms

### Catalog
A JSON file organized by language and major functional area (e.g., `translations/en/tomography.json`). Each catalog contains translation keys mapped to messages and optional descriptions.

**See:** `docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md` §3

### Source Text
The mandatory English text supplied as the second positional argument to `tr()`. It provides a last-resort fallback if the translation cannot be resolved through the catalogs. The English catalog remains the authoritative source.

**See:** `docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md` §5

### Translation Key
A stable semantic identifier describing meaning or intent (e.g., `tomography.segmentation.failed`). It must not depend on the displayed text or a specific UI widget.

**See:** `docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md` §4

### TranslationCatalogRegistry
The component that locates, loads, and caches translation catalogs by language and functional area.

**See:** `docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md` §2

### TranslationService
The component that resolves translation keys, processes message parameters and pluralization, manages the active language, applies fallback rules, and notifies the interface when the language changes.

**See:** `docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md` §2

### TranslationValidator
The component that checks catalog structure, key coverage, source-text consistency, formatting parameters, and translation completeness.

**See:** `docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md` §2`, §9

---

## 9. Deprecated and Retired Terms

The following terms appeared in earlier drafts and are **retired**. They must not be reintroduced.

### EditorHost
A component that was once proposed to manage a list of Editors inside an Area. **Removed by ADR-0001.** The Area manages its Editors directly via its internal Editor Layout Tree.

**Do not use.** If you encounter `EditorHost` in a document, treat it as a documentation error and correct it.

### Patient_Config_Manager
A proposed component that would independently own the patient lifecycle. **Removed by ADR-0004 and `project_pt.md` §3.** Patient lifecycle is managed through the Project Manager.

**Do not use.**

### PatientManager
A proposed application-level manager for the Patient. **Removed by ADR-0004.** There is no separate Patient lifecycle manager.

**Do not use.**

### Main Toolbar
A term that appeared in earlier drafts of `toolbar.md` to describe an application-level toolbar. In the current architecture, Toolbars are internal to Editors. The Top Bar is a structural component of the Workspace, not a "Main Toolbar".

**Do not use.** Prefer *Top Bar* for the structural component, and *Toolbar* for the Editor-internal widget.

### Panel
A term that appeared in earlier drafts. In the current architecture, there is only **Area** (structural container) and **Editor** (functional unit). A "Panel" is a semantic specialization of an Editor (`editor_kind = PANEL`), not a separate concept.

**Do not use as a structural term.** If needed, refer to an Editor whose kind is `PANEL`.

---

## 11. Maintenance

This glossary is a **living document**. When a new term is introduced in a specification:

1. Add it to the appropriate section here.
2. Cross-reference it from the document that defines it in detail.
3. If the term replaces a deprecated term, add the deprecated term to §9.

When a term's meaning changes:

1. Update the definition here **first**.
2. Update every document that uses the term.
3. Record the change in the CHANGELOG.

The glossary is not a substitute for the detailed contracts in the individual documents. It is a navigation aid and a consistency anchor. When in doubt, the detailed contract in the specific document governs.

# CranioZ Principles

## A. Project Philosophy
1. Internationalization from the Start
2. Open Source with a Clear LGPD Boundary

## B. Architecture
3. Extensibility by Design
4. Domain Knows No Infrastructure
5. Every Persistent Domain-State Mutation Is a Command
6. Single Source of Truth for Patient Data
7. Panels Are Areas
8. Independent Modules with Explicit Prerequisites
9. Clinical Semantics Are Explicit

## C. Clinical Quality
10. Consolidated Libraries Before Reinvention
11. Validation Before High-Impact Operations
12. Determinism and Reproducibility
13. Performance Is a Clinical Requirement
14. Clinical Logic Is Explicit and Testable

## D. Engineering Practice
15. Documentation Is Part of the Code
16. One Way to Do Each Thing
17. Errors Are Information, Not Failures
18. Active clinical data should remain GPU-resident whenever repeated rendering or computation makes transfer avoidance beneficial.

======================================================================
FILE: docs\adr\ADR-0001_Area_and_Editor.md
======================================================================

Status: Accepted
Date: 2026-10-12
Deciders: Architecture team
Related: overview.md, workspace_en.md, workspace_pt.md, editors.md, workspace-implementation.md

## 1. Context
The architecture documents disagree about the relationship between Area and Editor.

The overview.md §5.4 states:

    "In CranioZ, Area and Editor refer to the same concept: a unit of the UI that occupies a position in the Workspace Layout tree and provides a specific functional experience. There is no separate EditorHost or Editor abstraction. A Toolbar is internal to the Area; it is declared and materialized by the Area itself."

The workspace_en.md §2 states:

    "An Area is a container of Editors. It hosts one or more Editors, organized in its own internal Editor Layout Tree."

The workspace_pt.md §2.1 states:

    "Hospeda um EditorHost (que, por sua vez, hospeda um ou mais Editores)."

The editors.md §3 states:

    "Area and Editor are distinct concepts, but they share responsibilities through composition."

The workspace-implementation.md Phase 2 states:

    "EditorHost manages a list of Editors, with one active."

The prototype (Prototipagem_UI_06.pdf) shows:

* Areas labeled Area Left, Area Central, Area Right, Area Bottom.
* Each Area hosting one or more Editors (via tabs at the bottom of the Area).
* The Central Area can be split horizontally and vertically, hosting multiple Editors simultaneously (e.g., 2×2 grid of MPR viewers).

The contradiction: three incompatible readings exist:
* Area = Editor (single concept, no EditorHost).
* Area ⊃ EditorHost ⊃ Editor (Area is a structural container; EditorHost manages Editors).
* Area ⊃ Editor (Area directly hosts Editors, no EditorHost).

This ADR resolves the contradiction.

Decision:
* Area and Editor are distinct concepts.
* Area is a structural container provided by the Workspace. It occupies a position in the Area Layout Tree and hosts one or more Editors.
* Editor is a functional unit provided by the system, a Module, or a Plugin. It provides a specific interaction experience (Viewport3D, MPR Viewer, Console, Properties, etc.).
* An Area hosts Editors. The Central Area may be split horizontally and vertically, hosting multiple Editors simultaneously in an internal Editor Layout Tree.
* There is no EditorHost component.
* The EditorHost mentioned in earlier drafts (workspace_pt.md §2.1, workspace-implementation.md Phase 2) is removed. The Area itself manages its Editors directly.
* The overview.md §5.4 statement is corrected: Area and Editor are not the same concept. The correct statement is that they are complementary: Area provides where, Editor provides what.

## 3. Rationale
### 3.1 The prototype demands the distinction
The prototype shows:

Area Left, Area Central, Area Right, Area Bottom as structural regions.

The Central Area split into a 2×2 grid, hosting four different Editors (Axial MPR, Sagittal MPR, Coronal MPR, 3D Viewport).
Tabs at the bottom of each Area to switch between Editors.

If Area = Editor, then:
* The Central Area would have to be four different Areas (one per quadrant), losing the "Central" identity.
* The tabs at the bottom would have to belong to four different entities.
* The Region concept (a geometric grouping of Areas) would lose its meaning.
* The distinction is required by the design.

### 3.2 The Region concept requires Areas to be containers
The workspace_en.md §7.2 defines Region as "a geometric area derived from the Area Layout Tree that groups related Areas." If Area = Editor, then Regions group Editors, and the concept of "Central Region" becomes "group of Editors that happen to be in the center."

With the distinction, Regions group Areas, and an Area may host multiple Editors. The Central Region can contain one Area (Central) hosting a 2×2 grid of Editors. This matches the prototype.

### 3.3 The EditorLayoutSpec requires the distinction
The workspace_en.md §3.5 defines:

    python
    @dataclass(frozen=True)
    class EditorLayoutSpec:
        type: LayoutType                          # LEAF | SPLIT
        direction: SplitDirection | None = None   # HORIZONTAL | VERTICAL
        children: tuple["EditorLayoutSpec", ...] = ()
        editor_id: str | None = None              # when type == LEAF

This describes an internal tree of Editors inside an Area. If Area = Editor, this structure has no place to live. The distinction gives it a home.

### 3.4 The Editor lifecycle requires the distinction
editors.md §4 defines a lifecycle for Editors:


    instantiated → registered → inserted → activated ⇄ deactivated → removed → disposed
And a separate lifecycle for Areas (workspace_en.md §2.3):

    created → registered → placed → activated → deactivated → unregistered → destroyed

If Area = Editor, these two lifecycles must be merged. The merge would be awkward: an Editor is created by the EditorRegistry, inserted into an Area, activated, deactivated, removed, disposed — while the Area goes through its own lifecycle. Keeping them separate is cleaner.

### 3.5 Toolbars, Tools, and EditorContext belong to Editors
editors.md §10, §11, and §15 define:

* Toolbar is internal to an Editor.
* Tools are referenced by Toolbars.
* EditorContext is provided to an Editor.

If Area = Editor, then all of these become properties of Areas, which contradicts the Workspace's single rule (workspace_en.md §1.2):

"The Workspace manages Areas and Overlays... it does not own clinical state, Tools, Toolbars, or Module behavior."

The Workspace must not know about Toolbars or Tools. If Areas were Editors, and Editors have Toolbars, then the Workspace would indirectly own Toolbars. The distinction preserves the rule.

## 4. Consequences
## 4.1 Positive consequences
The prototype is consistent. Areas as containers, Editors as content, tabs at the bottom of each Area, 2×2 grid inside the Central Area.

Regions group Areas, not Editors. The Central Region can contain one Area (Central) with a complex internal Editor Layout Tree.

Two Editors of the same type may coexist. Within a single Area, each Editor must have a unique `editor_id`. A user who wants two Viewport3D
Editors in the same Area registers them under distinct editor_ids (e.g. `editor.viewport_3d.left` and `editor.viewport_3d.right`), both
pointing to the same Editor class in the EditorRegistry.

`Area.add_editor` rejects duplicate editor_ids with a clear `AreaError`. The LayoutBuilder propagates that error without swallowing it.

* The EditorLayoutSpec has a home — inside the Area.
* The Workspace remains ignorant of Toolbars, Tools, and Editor behavior.
* Modules declare Editor IDs, not Editor classes. The EditorRegistry resolves them.

### 4.2 Negative consequences
* The overview.md §5.4 must be corrected. The statement "Area and Editor are the same concept" is wrong.
* The EditorHost is removed. The workspace_pt.md §2.1, workspace-implementation.md Phase 2, and the folder structure must be updated.
* The Area must manage its Editors directly. The Area exposes methods to add, remove, and activate Editors, and to host the internal Editor Layout Tree.
* Two parallel lifecycles must be documented clearly. The Area lifecycle and the Editor lifecycle are related but distinct. The editors.md §4 and workspace_en.md §2.3 must be cross-referenced.


======================================================================
FILE: docs\adr\ADR-0002_EditorContext_and_Toolbar_Materialization.md
======================================================================

Status: Accepted
Date: 2026-10-13
Deciders: Architecture team
Related: ADR-0001_Area_and_Editor.md, editors.md, workspace_en.md,
         workspace-implementation.md, tools.md, toolbar.md

---

# 1. Context

Two decisions shape how the Workspace materializes a `ModuleUISpec`:

1. How many `EditorContext` instances exist, and what is shared between
   them.
2. Who creates the `Toolbar` widgets — the `LayoutBuilder` or the
   `LayoutRenderer`.

These decisions are interdependent. Both determine what "materialize"
means in the Workspace, and both affect whether the `LayoutBuilder`
remains a pure, Qt-free composition step. They are recorded together
for that reason.

---

## 1.1 EditorContext

The `EditorContext` is the controlled set of dependencies an Editor
receives at insertion time. The architecture document (`editors.md`
§15) already defines its intended shape:

    EditorContext
    ├── SceneQuery
    ├── SelectionService
    ├── CommandDispatcher
    ├── EventSubscription
    ├── ViewStateStore
    └── EditorServices

Two incompatible readings existed:

**Reading A — Shared context.**
A single `EditorContext` instance is created and passed to every
Editor in the composition.

**Reading B — One context per Editor.**
Each Editor receives its own `EditorContext` instance, with its own
`editor_id` and its own `ViewStateStore`.

Reading A is simpler to implement but breaks well-documented
invariants:

- `ViewStateStore` is explicitly per-Editor in `editors.md` §16.
- ADR-0001 states that two `Viewport3DEditor` instances are two
  independent instances.
- `EditorContext.editor_id` would have no consistent value if the same
  instance were shared among multiple Editors.

Reading B preserves those invariants, but raises a new question: if
`SceneQuery`, `CommandDispatcher` and `EventSubscription` are the same
for every Editor, creating a fresh copy per Editor would duplicate
expensive, global resources.

Neither reading alone is correct.

## 1.2 Toolbar materialization

A `Toolbar` is a Qt widget internal to an Editor (ADR-0002 is the
present document; see also `toolbar.md` and `tools.md`). It is
declared by a `ToolbarSpec` and materialized with the help of a
`ToolRegistry` and a `ThemeService`.

Two incompatible readings existed:

**Reading A — LayoutBuilder creates the Toolbar widget.**
The `LayoutBuilder` resolves `editor.toolbar_specs()` and creates
the Qt widget. The `LayoutRenderer` only places it.

**Reading B — LayoutRenderer creates the Toolbar widget.**
The `LayoutBuilder` produces pure data. The `LayoutRenderer`
materializes both the Editor's `content_widget()` and the
`Toolbar` widgets declared by `editor.toolbar_specs()`.

Reading A contradicts the contract of the `LayoutBuilder`, which is
defined as a pure composition step (`workspace-implementation.md`,
Phase 5):

> LayoutBuilder consumes a ModuleUISpec and produces a LayoutTree.

It also forces the `LayoutBuilder` to depend on Qt, requires a
`QApplication` for its tests, and duplicates the `LayoutRenderer`'s
responsibility of materializing widgets.

Reading B keeps the `LayoutBuilder` pure and gives the
`LayoutRenderer` sole responsibility for widget materialization. The
cost is that the `LayoutRenderer` must receive a `ToolRegistry` and a
`ThemeService` — which it needs anyway to materialize Toolbars.

---

# 2. Decision

## 2.1 EditorContext — hybrid model

A single `EditorContext` instance is created **per Editor**.

Within each instance:

- Fields whose values are **specific** to the Editor (identity,
  presentation state, per-Editor configuration) are isolated. Each
  Editor receives its own value or its own new instance.

- Fields whose values are **shared** between Editors (read-only
  queries, global dispatchers, event buses) hold a **reference to the
  same object**. They are not duplicated.

Conceptually:

    EditorContext (one per Editor)
    ├── editor_id            ── specific
    ├── extras               ── specific (copy)
    ├── view_state           ── specific (new instance)
    │
    ├── scene_query          ── shared (reference)
    ├── command_dispatcher   ── shared (reference)
    ├── event_subscription   ── shared (reference)
    └── editor_services      ── shared (reference)

This is a shallow copy with per-Editor overrides: the container is new
per Editor; the shared references are not copied.

## 2.2 Toolbar materialization — LayoutRenderer

The `LayoutBuilder` does **not** create Qt widgets. It produces pure
domain structures: `Area`, `Editor`, `LayoutTree`, `AreaManager`, and
`BuildResult`.

The `LayoutRenderer` materializes **all** widgets:

- One `Area` widget per `Area`.
- One `QSplitter` per `Split`.
- One Editor widget per `Editor`, obtained via
  `editor.content_widget()`.
- One `Toolbar` widget per `ToolbarSpec` returned by
  `editor.toolbar_specs()`.

The `LayoutBuilder` never imports `PySide6` and never requires a
`QApplication`.

## 2.3 Extension point

The `LayoutBuilder.build()` method accepts an optional
`shared_context: dict[str, Any]` parameter. Its values are propagated
by reference into every `EditorContext` created during that build.

The `LayoutBuilder` does not interpret the contents of
`shared_context`. Deciding what belongs there is the responsibility of
the caller (typically the `Workspace`).

This provides a stable extension point for future shared services
(`SceneQuery`, `CommandDispatcher`, `EventSubscription`) without
changing the `LayoutBuilder` contract when those services are
implemented.

---

# 3. Rationale

## 3.1 The hybrid model preserves invariants without duplicating resources

Reading A (shared context) breaks three documented invariants:

- `ViewStateStore` per Editor (`editors.md` §16).
- Two `Viewport3DEditor` instances are independent (ADR-0001).
- `EditorContext.editor_id` has a single, unambiguous value.

Reading B (one context per Editor, fully isolated) preserves those
invariants but duplicates global resources (`SceneQuery`, dispatchers,
event subscriptions). Those resources are the same for every Editor,
are often expensive to build, and are read-only for the Editor.

The hybrid model preserves the invariants of Reading B while
sharing the resources of Reading A. It is not a compromise: it is the
correct application of two distinct rules to two distinct kinds of
field.

## 3.2 The classification rule is explicit

The rule that distinguishes shared fields from specific fields is
stated once, in this ADR, and can be applied to every future field of
`EditorContext`:

A field is **shared** when **all** of the following are true:

1. Its value is immutable to the Editor, or mutations are
   thread-safe by design.
2. Its value is global to the project or session, not to the Editor.
3. Building it is expensive.
4. Two Editors seeing the same value is correct, not a bug.

A field is **specific** when **any** of the following is true:

1. It holds mutable state that must not leak between Editors.
2. It is identified by `editor_id`.
3. It is cheap to build.
4. Two Editors sharing it would be a bug.

This rule eliminates case-by-case debate. New fields are classified by
inspecting their nature, not by preference.

## 3.3 The LayoutBuilder must remain pure

The `LayoutBuilder` is defined as a pure composition step. Making it
create Qt widgets would:

- Force a `QApplication` for its tests, slowing them and making them
  brittle.
- Duplicate the `LayoutRenderer`'s responsibility.
- Split the widget lifecycle across two classes, with no single owner
  of creation and disposal.
- Violate the layering rule that separates composition logic from
  presentation.
- Serialize development: today, the `LayoutBuilder` is testable
  before the `LayoutRenderer` exists.

Keeping the `LayoutBuilder` Qt-free preserves all of these properties
at the cost of adding a `ToolRegistry` and a `ThemeService` to the
`LayoutRenderer` constructor — which it needs anyway.

## 3.4 Toolbars belong to the Editor

ADR-0002 (this document) and ADR-0001 establish that a Toolbar is
internal to an Editor, not to an Area and not to the Workspace. The
Editor declares its Toolbars via `toolbar_specs()`. The
materialization is performed by the component that materializes the
Editor — the `LayoutRenderer`. Ownership and materialization are
therefore consistent: the Editor declares, the `LayoutRenderer`
materializes.

The Workspace never hosts a Toolbar widget. The Area never hosts a
Toolbar widget.

## 3.5 Hybrid and pure-builder combine cleanly

The hybrid `EditorContext` and the pure `LayoutBuilder` reinforce each
other:

- The builder creates one context per Editor, isolating specifics.
- The builder propagates shared resources by reference via
  `shared_context`, without interpreting them.
- The builder never touches Qt, so it never needs to know how Toolbars
  are materialized.
- The renderer materializes widgets and consumes the same
  `EditorContext` instances the builder produced.

Neither decision depends on the other, but together they form the
complete answer to "how does a `ModuleUISpec` become a live UI?".

---

# 4. Consequences

## 4.1 Positive consequences

- **Documented invariants are preserved.** `ViewStateStore` is per
  Editor. Two Editors with the same `editor_id` are independent.
  `editor_id` on the context always refers to its own Editor.

- **Global resources are not duplicated.** `SceneQuery`,
  `CommandDispatcher` and `EventSubscription` are references, not
  copies. Cost is constant regardless of how many Editors exist.

- **`LayoutBuilder` remains pure.** No Qt import. No `QApplication`
  needed for its tests. They run in milliseconds.

- **`LayoutRenderer` owns widget lifecycle.** One place creates, one
  place disposes. The widget cache is rebuildable from the tree at any
  time.

- **Development can proceed in parallel.** The `LayoutBuilder` and
  its tests are complete before the `LayoutRenderer` exists.

- **Extension point is stable.** Adding `SceneQuery` later requires no
  interface change: it simply becomes a key in `shared_context`.

- **Classification rule is explicit and reusable.** Future fields of
  `EditorContext` are classified by a documented rule, not by
  preference.

## 4.2 Negative consequences

- **`LayoutRenderer` gains dependencies.** It must receive a
  `ToolRegistry` and a `ThemeService` in its constructor. This is
  expected: materializing a Toolbar requires resolving Tools and
  icons.

- **`EditorContext` is not a single object.** Callers must understand
  that the container is per Editor and that some fields are shared.
  The classification rule (§3.2) and the type structure make this
  explicit.

- **`shared_context` is untyped at the builder boundary.** The
  builder accepts `dict[str, Any]` and does not validate its contents.
  This is intentional (the builder must not know what services exist),
  but it means the caller is responsible for passing a coherent set.
  When the shared services are implemented, a stronger type
  (`SharedContext` dataclass) can replace the dict without changing
  the builder's behavior.

- **`extras` must be copied per Editor.** If passed by reference
  without copying, mutations in one Editor leak into others. The
  builder performs `dict(extras)` for each context. This is a
  documented responsibility of the builder, not an accident.

## 4.3 Neutral consequences

- **Field-by-field classification takes thought.** Every time a new
  field is added to `EditorContext`, the author must classify it
  according to §3.2. This is a small, deliberate cost — and it
  prevents the field from being added thoughtlessly.

---

# 5. Rules applied by this ADR

The following invariants are enforced by this decision:

1. Each Editor receives its own `EditorContext` instance.
2. `EditorContext.editor_id` always refers to its own Editor.
3. `EditorContext.extras` is copied per Editor; mutations do not leak.
4. `ViewStateStore` (and any future presentation state) is per Editor.
5. Shared services (`SceneQuery`, `CommandDispatcher`,
   `EventSubscription`) are propagated by reference, not copied.
6. The `LayoutBuilder` does not import Qt and does not require a
   `QApplication`.
7. The `LayoutBuilder` does not create widgets of any kind.
8. The `LayoutRenderer` is the sole materializer of widgets — Areas,
   Splits, Editors, and Toolbars.
9. A `Toolbar` widget is created by the `LayoutRenderer` from the
   `ToolbarSpec` returned by `editor.toolbar_specs()`.
10. The `LayoutBuilder.build()` accepts an optional `shared_context`
    mapping and propagates its values by reference into each
    `EditorContext`.
11. The `LayoutBuilder` does not interpret the contents of
    `shared_context`.
12. The classification rule in §3.2 governs every future addition to
    `EditorContext`.

---

# 6. Alternatives considered

## 6.1 A single shared EditorContext (Reading A for decision 1)

Rejected. Breaks `ViewStateStore`, breaks `editor_id` semantics,
breaks the independence of multiple Editors with the same
`editor_id`.

## 6.2 Fully isolated EditorContext (Reading B for decision 1)

Rejected. Duplicates global resources, which are expensive and
identical for every Editor. The hybrid model achieves the same
isolation for specifics without paying this cost.

## 6.3 LayoutBuilder creates Toolbar widgets (Reading A for decision 2)

Rejected. Breaks the purity of the `LayoutBuilder`. Forces a Qt
dependency into a class that is defined as Qt-free. Duplicates
materialization logic. Splits the widget lifecycle across two classes.

## 6.4 Editor materializes its own Toolbar widget

Considered and rejected as a general rule. Having each Editor create
its own `Toolbar` widget would push the `ToolRegistry` and
`ThemeService` dependencies into every Editor. The `LayoutRenderer`
already has these dependencies for its own purposes and is the
natural owner of widget creation. Editors declare; the renderer
materializes.

An Editor may still provide a convenience method that returns a fully
built widget, if a future Editor has unusual needs. This ADR does not
forbid it; it simply does not make it the default.

---

# 7. Example

## 7.1 The LayoutBuilder creates contexts (pure)

    shared = {
        "scene_query": request.scene_query,
        "command_dispatcher": request.command_dispatcher,
        "event_subscription": request.event_subscription,
    }

    for editor_id in editor_ids:
        editor = self._registry.create(
            editor_id,
            editor_id=editor_id,
            title=editor_id,
        )
        context = EditorContext(
            editor_id=editor_id,        # specific
            extras=dict(extras),        # specific (copy)
            view_state=ViewStateStore(),# specific (new)
            **shared,                   # shared (references)
        )
        editor.initialize(context)

No Qt import. No widget creation. The builder produces only domain
structures and their contexts.

## 7.2 The LayoutRenderer materializes widgets

    def _render_editor(self, editor: Editor) -> QWidget:
        container = QWidget()
        layout = QVBoxLayout(container)

        for toolbar_spec in editor.toolbar_specs():
            toolbar = Toolbar(
                spec=toolbar_spec,
                registry=self._tool_registry,
                theme=self._theme_service,
                context=editor.tool_context,
            )
            layout.addWidget(toolbar)

        layout.addWidget(editor.content_widget())
        return container

The renderer owns the Toolbar lifecycle. The builder never sees it.

---

# 8. Summary

Two decisions, one principle:

> **Per-Editor isolation for what is specific; shared references for
> what is common; and a single owner for widget materialization.**

The `EditorContext` is a per-Editor container whose specific fields
are isolated and whose shared fields reference global services.

The `LayoutBuilder` produces pure data and never touches Qt. The
`LayoutRenderer` materializes every widget — Areas, Splits, Editors,
and Toolbars.

The hybrid model is not a compromise between two positions. It is the
precise application of two distinct rules to two distinct kinds of
field. The pure builder is not an optimization. It is what keeps the
composition step testable, layer-correct, and developable in parallel
with the presentation step.


======================================================================
FILE: docs\adr\ADR-0003_UI_Widgets_and_Editor_Overlays.md
======================================================================

Status: Proposed
Date: 2026-10-13
Deciders: Architecture team
Related: ADR-0001_Area_and_Editor.md, ADR-0002_EditorContext_and_Toolbar_Materialization.md,
         editors.md, tools.md, toolbar.md, workspace_en.md,
         Prototipagem_UI_07.pdf (página 15)

---

# 1. Context

The CranioZ UI prototype (Prototipagem_UI_07.pdf, page 15) presents a catalog of reusable visual elements: semantic buttons ("Continuar", "Concluído"), icon-based actions ("Expandir tela", "Fechar janela",
"Abrir pasta", "Ocultar/Exibir", "Puxar/Arrastar"), input controls (sliders, scroll bars), 3D manipulation controls (translation and rotation gizmos), and state indicators (active/inactive colors, collapsed/expanded triangles).

This catalog is not a screen. It is a specification of the visual language of CranioZ — the atoms that appear across multiple screens.

The architecture already provides homes for some of these elements:

- **Toolbars** (`toolbar.md`) present Tools inside an Editor.
- **Tools** (`tools.md`) represent user intentions
  (`tool.view.expand`, `tool.file.open_folder`).
- **ToolRegistry** resolves tool_ids to Tool implementations.
- **ThemeService** (`ui/theme/service.py`) resolves logical icon names.
- **Editor internal overlays** (`editors.md` §17) are floating elements   inside an Editor's content area (gizmos, crosshairs, measurements).

However, the architecture does **not** yet define where the following belong:

1. **Composite generic widgets** that are neither Toolbars nor Editor    overlays. Example: a primary action button (`Continuar`) with a    specific visual style, used in the `steps_panel` and likely in dialogs.
2. **3D manipulation gizmos** that overlay a Viewport. Example: translation and rotation controls shown in the prototype over the    3D model.
3. **Semantic colors** (active/inactive, success/warning/error) that    are currently outside the ThemeService's responsibility.

Without an explicit decision, three risks emerge:

- **Duplication.** The same button widget could be re-implemented in   the `steps_panel`, in dialogs, and in Editors, with slightly different behavior or styling.
- **Misplacement.** A Tool could be created for something that is actually a generic widget, or a widget could be created for
  something that is actually a Tool. The boundary is not yet documented.
- **Inconsistency.** Colors, spacings, and states could diverge across screens, since there is no single place to define them.

This ADR establishes the boundary and the homes for these elements.

---

# 2. Decision

CranioZ organizes reusable UI elements into **three distinct locations**, each with a specific purpose and rule set.

## 2.1 `src/cranioz/ui/widgets/` — Generic composite widgets

A **generic composite widget** is a reusable Qt widget that:

- Combines multiple Qt primitives (buttons, labels, layouts) into a   consistent visual element.
- Is agnostic of CranioZ domain concepts (no knowledge of Editor,   Area, Scene, Module, Flow).
- Consumes `ThemeService` for colors and icons.
- Is used in more than one context (or is clearly destined to be).

Structure:

    src/cranioz/ui/widgets/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── buttons/
    │   ├── __init__.py
    │   ├── primary_button.py      # e.g. "Continuar"
    │   └── completed_button.py    # e.g. "Concluído"
    ├── sliders/
    │   ├── __init__.py
    │   └── labeled_slider.py      # slider with label and value
    └── ...

Rules:

- A widget in `ui/widgets/` must NOT import anything from   `ui/workspace/`, `ui/editors/`, `ui/tools/`, or `modules/`.
- A widget in `ui/widgets/` MAY import from `ui/theme/` and from   `PySide6`.
- A widget in `ui/widgets/` MUST NOT contain clinical logic, domain   rules, or user intentions. It presents; it does not act.
- A widget in `ui/widgets/` is created **when its first concrete   reuse appears**, not before. Speculative creation is forbidden by   the project's rule "do not create abstractions before the need".

## 2.2 `src/cranioz/ui/editors/overlays/` — Internal overlays of an Editor

An **internal overlay** is a visual element that floats over an Editor's content, participates in hit-testing, and is owned by that
Editor. It is defined in `editors.md` §17.

Structure:

    src/cranioz/ui/editors/overlays/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── gizmos/
    │   ├── __init__.py
    │   ├── translation_gizmo.py   # cross with orthogonal arrows
    │   └── rotation_gizmo.py      # rotation arc
    ├── rulers/
    │   ├── __init__.py
    │   └── ruler_overlay.py       # measurement ruler
    └── ...

Rules:

- An overlay in `ui/editors/overlays/` MUST live inside the   `content_widget()` of the Editor that uses it.
- An overlay MUST NOT mutate clinical state directly. Interactive   overlays emit Commands through the Editor's `EditorContext`.
- An overlay MAY participate in hit-testing.
- An overlay is created **when the Editor that uses it is   implemented**, not before.

## 2.3 Icon-based actions — Tools, not widgets

An icon-based action in the prototype (e.g. "Expandir tela", "Fechar janela", "Abrir pasta", "Ocultar/Exibir", "Puxar/Arrastar") represents a **user intention**. In CranioZ, user intentions are
Tools (`tools.md`).

Therefore, an icon-based action is:

- Declared as a Tool in `src/cranioz/ui/tools/builtin/<category>/`.
- Registered in the `ToolRegistry`.
- Referenced by `tool_id` in a `ToolbarSpec`.
- Presented by a `Toolbar` widget, inside the Editor that declares the Toolbar.
- Resolved for its icon via `ThemeService.icon_name()`.

Structure:

    src/cranioz/ui/tools/builtin/
    ├── view/
    │   ├── expand.py              # tool.view.expand
    │   └── toggle_visibility.py   # tool.view.toggle_visibility
    ├── file/
    │   └── open_folder.py         # tool.file.open_folder
    ├── window/
    │   └── close.py               # tool.window.close
    └── interaction/
        └── drag_handle.py         # tool.interaction.drag_handle

Rules:

- An icon-based action MUST NOT be implemented as a widget in `ui/widgets/`. It is a Tool.
- An icon-based action MUST NOT be materialized ad-hoc by an Editor.
  It is materialized by a `Toolbar` from a `ToolbarSpec`.
- The Tool's `icon` attribute is a logical name. `ThemeService` resolves it to a concrete icon for the active theme.

## 2.4 Semantic colors — ThemeService

Colors that express state or meaning ("active" green, "inactive" gray, "success", "warning", "error") are **semantic colors**. They belong to `ThemeService`.

The ThemeService will be extended, when the first concrete use appears, with a method:

    def color(self, semantic_name: str) -> str:
        """Return the concrete color for a semantic role.

        Examples: "active", "inactive", "success", "warning", "error".
        """

Rules:

- No widget, Editor, or Toolbar may hardcode a semantic color.
  All semantic colors are resolved via `ThemeService.color()`.
- Non-semantic colors (transient layout constants) may be resolved locally but should eventually be moved to ThemeService if they become repeated.

## 2.5 Native Qt controls

Some elements in the prototype are simply native Qt controls and do not require a custom widget:

- Horizontal slider → `QSlider`
- Vertical and horizontal scroll bars → `QScrollBar` / `QScrollArea`
- Collapsed/expanded triangles → native tree/grouper indicators

Rules:

- A native Qt control is used directly, without an intermediate CranioZ widget, unless a specific composition (e.g. label + value
  + unit) is needed repeatedly.
- When a specific composition is needed, it becomes a widget in
  `ui/widgets/` and is created only at that point.

## 2.6 No premature creation

No folder under `ui/widgets/`, `ui/editors/overlays/`, or `ui/tools/builtin/<new_category>/` may be created in advance of a concrete need.

A folder is created together with its first widget or first tool.

Rationale: the same rule that governed the LayoutBuilder (ADR-0002 §2.3) governs widgets. Abstractions are created when the need is concrete, not speculatively.

---

# 3. Rationale

## 3.1 Three homes, three responsibilities

The three locations are not arbitrary. They correspond to three
distinct kinds of responsibility:

| Location | Responsibility | Depends on |
|---|---|---|
| `ui/widgets/` | Generic visual composition | Qt, ThemeService |
| `ui/editors/overlays/` | Editor-internal interaction layers | Qt, Editor's `EditorContext` |
| `ui/tools/builtin/` | User intentions | ToolRegistry (declaration) |

If these were merged into one folder, the boundaries between "generic presentation", "interactive overlay", and "user intention" would blur.
A generic button would sit next to a 3D rotation gizmo, and nobody would know where to add the next widget.

The three locations preserve the following invariants:

- **Widgets do not know the domain.** A `PrimaryButton` is the same button in the `steps_panel` and in a dialog. It has no idea what a   Flow is.
- **Overlays do not escape their Editor.** A translation gizmo belongs to the Editor that uses it. It is not a shared widget; another
  Editor that needs one declares its own.
- **Tools are declared, not materialized.** A `tool.view.expand` is a declaration. The widget is created by the Toolbar, from the Tool contract. The Tool does not create its own widget.

## 3.2 The prototype does not dictate the structure

The prototype (page 15) presents a visual catalog. It does not say where each element lives. That is an architectural decision, and this ADR makes it.

The catalog is used as evidence for **which** elements exist, not for **where** they belong. The mapping from catalog to location is established by this ADR, following the existing architectural principles.

## 3.3 Consistency with ADR-0001 and ADR-0002

- **ADR-0001** establishes that a Toolbar is internal to an Editor.
  This ADR extends that: an icon-based action is a Tool, presented by a Toolbar inside an Editor. The Tool does not become an Area or a Workspace element.
- **ADR-0002** establishes that the `LayoutRenderer` materializes widgets and that `EditorContext` is per-Editor (hybrid). This ADR extends that: overlays inside an Editor belong to that Editor's
  `content_widget()`, not to the `LayoutRenderer`. The `LayoutRenderer` materializes Areas, Splits, Editors, and Toolbars;
  it does not materialize gizmos or overlay widgets.

## 3.4 Avoiding three common anti-patterns

Without this ADR, the project would naturally drift into three anti-patterns:

**Anti-pattern 1 — Widget spam.**
Every Editor creates its own buttons, its own sliders, its own styling. The UI becomes visually inconsistent.

**Anti-pattern 2 — Tool/widget confusion.**
A "toggle visibility" action could be implemented as a widget in `ui/widgets/` instead of a Tool. It would then be un-discoverable by the ToolRegistry and would not participate in the Toolbar mechanism.

**Anti-pattern 3 — Gizmo leakage.**
A 3D rotation gizmo could be placed in `ui/widgets/` and reused between an Editor and, say, a settings dialog. This would break the overlay's ownership contract and couple unrelated contexts.

The three homes prevent all three.

## 3.5 The "no premature creation" rule

The project has consistently applied the rule "do not create abstractions before the need". This is visible in:

- `EditorContext` (ADR-0002 §2.3): `shared_context` is accepted but not propagated until shared services exist.
- `LayoutBuilder` (ADR-0002 §2.2): does not materialize widgets until the `LayoutRenderer` exists.
- `EditorContext` fields (ADR-0002 §3.2): added only when the corresponding service is implemented.

This ADR applies the same rule to widgets, overlays, and Tools. No folder is created speculatively. Each folder appears with its first concrete member.

---

# 4. Consequences

## 4.1 Positive consequences

- **Clear homes for every UI element.** Every element in the prototype catalog has exactly one architectural home, documented here.
- **No duplication.** A `PrimaryButton` is created once and reused.
  A `tool.view.expand` is declared once and referenced by any Toolbar.
- **Consistent visual language.** Semantic colors, spacings, and states flow through `ThemeService`, not through ad-hoc local code.
- **Discoverable tools.** All icon-based actions are Tools, visible to the `ToolRegistry`. They can be discovered by menus, Toolbars,
  automation, and future MCP integration.
- **Overlay ownership is preserved.** Gizmos belong to the Editor that uses them. No cross-Editor leakage.
- **Parallel development.** Each location can be developed in parallel once its first member is needed. No folder blocks another.
- **Aligned with existing ADRs.** This ADR does not contradict ADR-0001 or ADR-0002. It extends them.

## 4.2 Negative consequences

- **Discipline is required.** A developer who needs a button must decide whether it belongs in `ui/widgets/` or is a Tool. This
  requires reading the rules in §2.
- **First-reuse decision is a judgment call.** "Used in more than one context" is a heuristic, not a mechanical rule. The ADR gives
  guidelines but not a decision procedure. When in doubt, the developer should prefer local implementation first and promote only on real reuse.
- **ThemeService must grow.** Semantic colors require expanding `ThemeService`. This is a future change, not part of this ADR.
- **No immediate impact.** This ADR creates no code today. It documents rules that apply to future work. This is a benefit, not a drawback, but it means the ADR has no immediate test coverage.

## 4.3 Neutral consequences

- **Widgets and Tools are separated, even when they look similar.** A button and a Tool may look identical on screen. Architecturally they are different: a button is presented by a Toolbar and resolved
  by the ToolRegistry; a widget is presented directly by a parent widget. This is intentional.

---

# 5. Rules applied by this ADR

1. Generic composite widgets live in `src/cranioz/ui/widgets/`.
2. Widgets in `ui/widgets/` do not import from `ui/workspace/`,
   `ui/editors/`, `ui/tools/`, or `modules/`.
3. Widgets in `ui/widgets/` present; they do not act on the domain.
4. Editor-internal overlays live in
   `src/cranioz/ui/editors/overlays/`.
5. Overlays live inside the `content_widget()` of the Editor that
   uses them.
6. Overlays emit Commands; they do not mutate clinical state directly.
7. Icon-based actions are Tools in
   `src/cranioz/ui/tools/builtin/<category>/`.
8. Icon-based actions are presented by a Toolbar from a `ToolbarSpec`; they are not materialized ad-hoc.
9. Tool `icon` attributes are logical names, resolved by
   `ThemeService.icon_name()`.
10. Semantic colors are provided by `ThemeService.color()`.
11. No widget, Editor, or Toolbar hardcodes a semantic color.
12. Native Qt controls are used directly when no specific composition is needed.
13. A folder under `ui/widgets/`, `ui/editors/overlays/`, or
    `ui/tools/builtin/<new_category>/` is created only together with its first member.
14. No folder is created speculatively.

---

# 6. Alternatives considered

## 6.1 A single `ui/widgets/` folder for everything

Rejected. Would mix generic widgets, Editor overlays, and icon-based actions. Would blur responsibilities and violate the principle that overlays belong to their Editor.

## 6.2 A `ui/atoms/` folder with all visual primitives

Rejected. "Atom" is not an architectural concept in CranioZ. It would require a separate design language documentation and would not align with the existing structure (Toolbars, Tools, Editor overlays).

## 6.3 Icon-based actions as widgets, not Tools

Rejected. It would remove these actions from the ToolRegistry, making them undiscoverable by menus, automation, and future MCP. It would also violate `tools.md`, which establishes that user intentions are Tools.

## 6.4 All overlays as Workspace Overlays

Rejected. Workspace Overlays (`workspace.md` §7) are floating layers over a Region of Areas. Gizmos inside a Viewport are internal to the Editor and belong to its content. Conflating the two would break the Workspace's single rule (`workspace.md` §1.2).

## 6.5 Gizmos as generic widgets in `ui/widgets/`

Rejected. A gizmo is bound to a specific Editor's content area and participates in its hit-testing. It is not a generic widget.

---

# 7. Example

## 7.1 `Continuar` button in `steps_panel`

The `steps_panel` (a Workspace Overlay) needs a `Continuar` button.

- The button is a **generic widget**: it is used in the `steps_panel` and will likely appear in dialogs. It presents a primary action.
- It lives in `src/cranioz/ui/widgets/buttons/primary_button.py`.
- It consumes `ThemeService` for the "active" semantic color (green).
- It does not know what a Flow is; the `steps_panel` handles the click and dispatches the appropriate application command.

## 7.2 "Fechar janela atual" in a Viewport Toolbar

A Viewport toolbar wants a "close window" action.

- The action is a **Tool**: `tool.window.close`.
- It lives in `src/cranioz/ui/tools/builtin/window/close.py`.
- It is registered in the `ToolRegistry` at boot.
- The Viewport Editor's `ToolbarSpec` references it by
  `tool_id="tool.window.close"`.
- The Toolbar widget resolves the Tool, calls `is_available` and
  `is_enabled`, and presents the icon.
- The Tool's `icon` attribute is `"close"`. `ThemeService` resolves it.

## 7.3 Rotation gizmo over a 3D model

A Viewport3D Editor needs a rotation gizmo.

- The gizmo is an **Editor internal overlay**.
- It lives in
  `src/cranioz/ui/editors/overlays/gizmos/rotation_gizmo.py`.
- It is instantiated by the `Viewport3DEditor` inside its
  `content_widget()`.
- On interaction, it emits a Command through the Editor's `EditorContext`; it does not mutate the Scene directly.
- The Workspace and the LayoutRenderer never see the gizmo; only the Editor's content widget does.

## 7.4 "Active" green in the `steps_panel`

The `steps_panel` shows completed steps in green.

- The color is a **semantic color**: "active" / "completed".
- It is resolved via `ThemeService.color("active")`.
- The `steps_panel` does not hardcode `#00AA00` or similar.
- Future themes (e.g. high-contrast) can change the color without changing the panel.

---



======================================================================
FILE: docs\adr\ADR-0004_Patient_Domain.md
======================================================================

Status: Accepted
Date: 2026-10-13
Deciders: Architecture team
Related: ADR-0001_Area_and_Editor.md, ADR-0002_EditorContext_and_Toolbar_Materialization.md,
         ADR-0003_UI_Widgets_and_Editor_Overlays.md,
         project_pt.md, patient.md, Prototipagem_UI_08.pdf

---

# 1. Context

The Patient domain is the clinical subject of CranioZ. A Patient is
the person associated with the imaging, planning, and documents of
a case. The domain model for the Patient must be decided before the
Patient module is implemented, because the model drives:

- The structure of the `cranioz.domain.patient` package.
- The contract between the domain and the application layer.
- The mapping between the domain and the Patient module UI.
- The persistence format consumed by the Project Repository.
- The relationship between the Patient and other domain entities
  (imaging, documents, odontogram, photographs).

The Patient module UI (Prototipagem_UI_08.pdf) presents the patient
record as **nine functional sections**:

1. Identificação
2. Documentos e Contato
3. Filiação
4. Endereço
5. Queixa e História
6. Comorbidades
7. Medicamentos e Alergias
8. Antecedentes (with four subsections: cirúrgico, dispositivos e
   implantes, familiares, saúde da mulher)
9. Hábitos e Vícios

Three design questions had to be answered before implementation:

1. **Composition.** Should the Patient be a single dataclass with
   ~40 fields, or should it be composed of value objects, one per
   functional section?
2. **Boundary.** Should the Patient carry references to the imaging,
   photographs, odontogram, and documents associated with the
   person, or should those entities be separated and reference the
   Patient?
3. **Conditional invariants.** Should rules that depend on fields in
   different sections (e.g., "women's health fields must be empty
   when the patient is not female") be enforced by each section, or
   by the aggregate?

The contradiction that motivated this ADR: the initial draft of the
Patient domain (piece 7.2) proposed a minimal model with a single
`medical_history: str` field and an `extras: dict` placeholder.
This model was insufficient for the sections required by the
prototype, and it invited a "god object" pattern. A decision was
needed on how to structure the domain correctly before expanding
it.

This ADR records the resolution.

---

# 2. Decision

## 2.1 Composition by sections

The Patient is **composed of nine value objects**, one per
functional section:

    Patient
    ├── identity: PatientIdentity (§1 Identificação)
    ├── documents: PatientDocuments (§2 Documentos e Contato)
    ├── filiation: PatientFiliation (§3 Filiação)
    ├── address: PatientAddress (§4 Endereço)
    ├── complaint: PatientComplaint (§5 Queixa e História)
    ├── comorbidities: PatientComorbidities (§6 Comorbidades)
    ├── medications: PatientMedications (§7 Medicamentos e Alergias)
    ├── antecedents: PatientAntecedents (§8 Antecedentes)
    └── habits: PatientHabits (§9 Hábitos e Vícios)


Each section is a frozen dataclass. Each section is testable in
isolation. Each section carries no knowledge of the other
sections.

The Patient itself is the aggregate root. It carries the
`PatientId`, the composition of the nine sections, and the
cross-section invariants.

## 2.2 Boundary: entities that reference the Patient

The following concepts do **not** belong to the Patient:

- Imaging volumes (DICOM, CT, CBCT)
- 3D meshes (STL, OBJ, PLY)
- Clinical photographs
- Odontogram (teeth, procedures)
- Documents (anamneses, prescriptions, certificates)
- Surgical planning data (osteotomies, splints, guides)

These entities belong to the Project, or to dedicated entities that
reference both the Patient and the Project. The relationship is
**inverted**: those entities reference the Patient, not the other
way around.

The Patient does not carry a list of them. It does not carry paths,
IDs, or references to them.

## 2.3 Cross-section invariants are enforced by the aggregate

Rules that depend on fields living in **different sections** are
enforced by the `Patient.__post_init__`, not by the sections
themselves.

Rules that depend only on fields inside a single section are
enforced by that section's `__post_init__`.

The distinction is deliberate: a section is responsible only for
the invariants it can verify in isolation. Any rule that needs
information from another section is an aggregate concern.

## 2.4 Value objects and enums

The domain defines the following value objects and enums:

| Name | Kind | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `PatientId` | value object | Stable identity of the patient. |
| `PatientSex` | enum | `MALE`, `FEMALE`, `OTHER`. |
| `PatientRace` | enum | IBGE categories. |
| `BloodType` | enum | Combined ABO + Rh. |
| `DocumentType` | enum | `RG`, `CPF`, `CNH`, `PASSPORT`, `OTHER`. |
| `PhoneKind` | enum | `MOBILE`, `HOME`, `WORK`, `OTHER`. |
| `PhoneNumber` | value object | Number + kind. |
| `ComorbidityCategory` | enum | 11 categories. |
| `PatientIdentity` ... `PatientHabits` | value objects | §1–§9. |
| `PatientSummary` | value object | Lightweight projection. |
| `Patient` | aggregate root | The patient itself. |

## 2.5 No `PatientManager`

Consistent with `project_pt.md` §3, there is no `PatientManager`.
The Patient is a domain entity referenced by a Project. The
patient's lifecycle is managed through the Project Manager, in the
context of the projects that reference the patient.

## 2.6 Age is computed, not stored

The Patient exposes `age(at_date: date | None = None) -> int | None`.
The value is derived from `identity.birth_date`. It is not stored.
This avoids a redundant field that would become stale.

---

# 3. Rationale

## 3.1 The prototype requires the structure

The Patient module UI presents the record as nine sections. A
single dataclass with ~40 fields would force the UI to know the
field list of every section, and would make partial updates
(e.g., saving only the §6 Comorbidades section) awkward. The
composed model matches the UI structure and makes partial updates
natural.

## 3.2 Composition prevents the "god object"

The naive reading of the prototype would put imaging, photographs,
odontogram, and documents *inside* the Patient. This is a known
anti-pattern: the entity accumulates data that is not intrinsic to
its identity. With composition and inversion:

- The Patient stays focused on the person.
- Each supporting entity (imaging, odontogram, document) references
  the Patient and lives on its own.
- The Patient's persistence format stays small and stable.

This mirrors the Object model (`objects.md`), where clinical
semantics and data representations are separated.

## 3.3 The aggregate is the right boundary for cross-section rules

A rule like "women's health fields must be empty when the patient
is not female" depends on `identity.sex` and
`antecedents.pregnancy_contraception`. If the rule lived in
`PatientAntecedents`, the section would need a reference to the
patient's sex, coupling the two sections. If the rule lived in
`PatientIdentity`, the section would need to know about the
antecedents, which is worse. The aggregate is the only boundary
that already knows about both, and it is the natural place for
rules that span sections.

## 3.4 Value objects are cheap and testable

Each section is a small frozen dataclass. Tests for a section do
not need to construct the whole Patient. Tests for the aggregate
focus on the cross-section invariants, not on the internal
validations of each section.

This keeps the test suite fast and the failures localized: when a
section validation fails, the failing test is about that section,
not about the Patient aggregate.

## 3.5 Consistency with existing ADRs

- **ADR-0001** establishes the Area / Editor distinction. This ADR
  applies the same principle to the domain: identity of the entity
  vs. content associated with it.
- **ADR-0002** establishes the hybrid context model for Editors.
  This ADR does not touch the UI layer; it stays in the domain.
- **ADR-0003** establishes the three homes for UI widgets. The
  Patient module UI will use those homes; the domain does not.

The ADR is therefore additive: it does not contradict any previous
decision.

---

# 4. Consequences

## 4.1 Positive consequences

- **Clear separation of concerns.** Each section has a single
  responsibility. Adding a field to one section does not affect the
  others.
- **Testable in isolation.** Each section and each enum is tested
  independently. The aggregate tests focus on cross-section rules.
- **The Patient stays a domain entity, not a container.** Imaging,
  photographs, odontogram, and documents are separate.
- **The UI structure mirrors the domain structure.** The nine
  sections of the prototype are the nine value objects of the
  domain.
- **Persistence is modular.** Each section can be serialized
  independently. A partial update touches only one section.
- **i18n is clean.** Each section's fields have stable identifiers
  for translation keys.
- **Future extensions are local.** Promoting a free-form field to a
  structured value object (e.g., turning `allergies: str` into a
  list of `Allergy` records) affects only that section.

## 4.2 Negative consequences

- **More files.** Nine sections plus nine enums plus value objects
  means ~18 files in `cranioz.domain.patient`. This is more files
  than a single dataclass would require. The trade-off is
  accepted: the files are small, focused, and testable.
- **Cross-section invariants live in the aggregate.** Developers
  must know that some rules are not in the section that appears to
  "own" the field. The ADR documents this clearly, and the
  aggregate is the only place that can enforce them.
- **Some redundancy between section and aggregate.** The aggregate
  must construct and validate the sections. This is minor
  boilerplate, not a design smell.

## 4.3 Neutral consequences

- **`extras: dict` is removed.** The initial draft had a placeholder
  `extras` dict. It is gone. If a future piece genuinely needs a
  free-form escape hatch, it will be reintroduced with a specific
  purpose.
- **`medical_history: str` is decomposed.** The initial draft had a
  single free-form field. It is replaced by eight fields across
  §5 (Queixa e História), §6 (Comorbidades), §7 (Medicamentos e
  Alergias), and §8 (Antecedentes).
- **The field names are in English in the code.** The prototype
  uses Portuguese labels. This is consistent with the rest of the
  codebase and the i18n approach.

---

# 5. Rules applied by this ADR

1. The Patient is composed of nine section value objects, one per
   functional section of the record.
2. Each section is a frozen dataclass, independent of the other
   sections.
3. The Patient is the aggregate root. It carries the `PatientId`,
   the sections, and the cross-section invariants.
4. Cross-section invariants are enforced by `Patient.__post_init__`,
   not by individual sections.
5. Imaging, meshes, photographs, odontogram, and documents are
   separate entities that reference the Patient. They are not part
   of the Patient.
6. There is no `PatientManager`. The Patient's lifecycle is managed
   through the Project Manager.
7. Age is computed from `birth_date`. It is not stored.
8. `PatientSummary` is a lightweight projection carrying only
   `patient_id`, `display_name`, `birth_date`, and `sex`.
9. `display_name` prefers `identity.social_name` and falls back to
   `identity.full_name`.
10. All value objects are frozen. The `Patient` aggregate is
    mutable, because it is the boundary through which updates are
    coordinated.
11. Field names are in English in the code. User-facing labels are
    localized via i18n.
12. The domain layer has no dependency on Qt, filesystem,
    persistence technology, or any infrastructure concern.

---

# 6. Alternatives considered

## 6.1 Single dataclass with ~40 fields

Rejected. Would force the UI to know the field list of every
section. Would make partial updates awkward. Would obscure the
structure that the prototype already establishes.

## 6.2 Composition with sections as nested dicts

Rejected. A dict loses the type information and makes validation
implicit. The section structure is known at design time; it should
