# Professional Module

**Module ID:** `professional`
**Status:** Proposed
**Scope:** Personal, administrative, and financial management of the surgeon
**Persistence:** Dedicated database outside projects (`~/.cranioz/professional/`)

---

## 1. Purpose

The **Professional Module** manages the surgeon's personal, administrative, and financial information. It is the application's administrative counterpart to the clinical modules.

The module does **not** handle patient clinical data (imaging, planning, osteotomies, Scene). It produces administrative artifacts — documents, budgets, appointments, procedure catalogs — that are **consumed** by clinical modules, particularly the Patient Module.

The module is **self-contained**: it works without any open project, and its data is reused across projects.

---

## 2. Core Principles

- **Outside projects.** Professional data lives in a dedicated database, not inside clinical projects.
- **Reusable across projects.** A procedure catalog, a document template, or a budget is defined once and reused for many patients.
- **Reference, do not contain, patient data.** The module references patients and projects by ID; it never stores clinical content.
- **Translate everything.** Specialties, categories, and procedures are always resolved through the `TranslationService`.
- **Commands for all mutations.** Every persistent change passes through an application Command.
- **Single service owns the database.** A `ProfessionalService` is the only component that reads or writes the professional database.

---

## 3. Ownership and Boundaries

### 3.1 What the Module Owns

- The professional's profile.
- The procedure catalog (specialties, categories, procedures).
- Document templates (orientations, contracts, consent terms, prescriptions, certificates).
- The appointment schedule.
- Budgets and budget items.
- Attachments (logo, signature image).

### 3.2 What the Module References (Does Not Own)

- Patients — referenced by `patient_id`, live in projects.
- Projects — referenced by `project_id`, live in the Project Root.
- Osteotomy patterns — a separate shared catalog (see ADR-0006).
- Scene, imaging, planning — never referenced by this module.

### 3.3 What the Module Does Not Do

- It does not create, modify, or delete patients or projects.
- It does not store clinical data (imaging, planning, Scene).
- It does not implement clinical rules.
- It does not manage the Project lifecycle.

---

## 4. Persistence

### 4.1 Location

```text
~/.cranioz/professional/          (Linux/macOS)
%APPDATA%\CranioZ\Professional\   (Windows)
```

The location is resolved by the same ConfigurationPaths component used for project_root, with an override in config/paths.json and an environment variable CRANIOZ_HOME.

### 4.2 Structure

    professional/
    ├── professional.db          # SQLite
    ├── professional.db.bak      # Automatic backup
    ├── templates/               # Document templates
    │   ├── orientations/
    │   ├── contracts/
    │   ├── consent_terms/
    │   ├── prescriptions/
    │   ├── certificates/
    │   └── declarations/
    ├── attachments/             # Logo, signature
    ├── translations/            # User-supplied translations for custom entities
    │   ├── pt-BR.json
    │   └── en.json
    └── exports/                 # Generated PDFs
        └── budgets/

### 4.3 Technology
SQLite. Chosen for relational integrity, indexed queries (schedule, budgets), ACID transactions, and versioned migrations. A single file simplifies backup and export.

### 4.4 Schema Versioning
A schema_version table tracks the current version. Migrations are applied on database open, following the pattern of ProjectMigrationService. Invalid or future schemas are rejected with a clear error.

## 5. Module Structure
The module is organized into five tabs, presented in a default order that follows the surgeon's natural workflow.

#	Tab	Purpose	Frequency
1	Professional	Personal and professional profile	Rare
2	Procedures	Catalog of procedures and prices	Occasional
3	Budgets	Budget creation from procedure catalog	Frequent
4	Schedule	Appointments, tasks, scheduled surgeries	Daily
5	Document Templates	Reusable document templates	Occasional
Default order rationale: identity → offering → billing → operation → delivery.

Reorganization: the user can reorder tabs, following the pattern of the Patient Module.

## 6. Tab: Professional
6.1 Purpose
The surgeon's identity and presentation data. Used in generated documents (budgets, contracts, prescriptions, orientations).

### 6.2 Fields
    Field	                    Type	                                    Required
    Salutation	                Enum (Dr., Dra., Prof., Profa., —)	        No
    Full name	                Text	                                    Yes
    Registration type	        Enum (CRM, CRO, CREFONO, OTHER)	            No
    Registration number	        Text	                                    No
    Email	                    Text	                                    No
    Phone	                    Text	                                    No
    Address	                    Text	                                    No
    City	                    Text	                                    No
    State	                    Text	                                    No
    Country	                    Text	                                    No
    Clinic logo	                File reference	                            No
    Digital signature	        File reference	                            No
    Date format	                Enum (DD/MM/YYYY, MM/DD/YYYY, YYYY-MM-DD)	Yes

### 6.3 Preview
A signature preview panel shows how the professional's identity will appear in generated documents, using the current profile fields.

### 6.4 Behavior
Saving dispatches an UpdateProfessionalProfileCommand.

Cancelling reverts the fields.

The onboarding wizard populates the minimal fields on first run; the tab is used for later edits.

## 7. Tab: Procedures
### 7.1 Purpose
The catalog of procedures offered by the surgeon. Serves as the basis for budgets.

### 7.2 Hierarchy

    Specialty
        └── Category
                └── Procedure

Specialties are built-in or user-created.

Categories group procedures within a specialty.

Procedures are the final entity.

### 7.3 Built-in Specialties
The following are provided as built-in, non-deletable suggestions:

Odontology (area)
Otorhinolaryngology
Oral and Maxillofacial Surgery
Plastic Surgery
Orthodontics
Implantology

The user may add custom specialties. Custom entities are always translatable (see §11).

### 7.4 Procedure Fields
Field	                    Type	                Required
Category	                FK	                    Yes
Name (translation key)	    Text	                Yes
Default price	            Decimal	                Yes
Default duration (minutes)	Integer                 No
TUSS code	                Text	                No
CID code	                Text	                No
Notes	                    Text	                No
Active	                    Boolean	                Yes

### 7.5 Actions
Create, edit, deactivate, and delete procedures, categories, and specialties. Deletion of a specialty or category cascades to its children after confirmation.

### 7.6 Catalog vs. Performed Procedures
The catalog holds definitions. Performed procedures (with a patient, date, and execution) live elsewhere:

Inside a budget, as line items.

Inside the schedule, as scheduled appointments.

Inside the Patient Module, as part of the patient's history.

The catalog never stores instances.

## 8. Tab: Budgets
### 8.1 Purpose
Creation of budgets for a specific patient, composed of procedures from the catalog.

### 8.2 Budget Fields
Field	                            Type	                                        Required
Patient	                            Reference (patient_id)	                        Yes
Project	                            Reference (project_id)	                        No
Status	                            Enum (draft, sent, accepted, rejected, expired)	Yes
Discount	                        Decimal	                                        No
Total	                            Decimal	                                        Computed
Valid until	                        Date	                                        No
Notes	                            Text	                                        No

### 8.3 Budget Item Fields
Field	                            Type	                            Required
Procedure	                        FK	                                Yes
Description	                        Text	                            No
Quantity	                        Integer	                            Yes (default 1)
Unit price	                        Decimal	                            Yes (default from procedure)
Notes	                            Text	                            No


### 8.4 Computation
    subtotal = Σ (quantity × unit_price)
    total    = subtotal − discount

### 8.5 State Machine
    draft ──▶ sent ──▶ accepted
                  ├──▶ rejected
                  └──▶ expired


Transitions are validated. accepted and rejected are terminal except for explicit reopening by the user, which resets the budget to draft.


### 8.6 PDF Export
Budgets can be exported to PDF. The PDF includes:

Header with logo, name, registration, address.

Patient identification.

Line items, subtotal, discount, total.

Validity date.

Signature image.

The PDF is saved in exports/budgets/ and, optionally, attached to the patient in the Patient Module.

### 8.7 Actions
Create, edit, duplicate, delete, export PDF, change status.

## 9. Tab: Schedule
### 9.1 Purpose
The surgeon's personal calendar: appointments, tasks, scheduled surgeries, and administrative notes. Independent of projects.

### 9.2 Views
Weekly (primary).

Monthly (overview).

List (upcoming appointments).

### 9.3 Appointment Types
    Type	        Description
    Consultation	Initial or follow-up visit
    Surgery	        Scheduled surgical procedure
    Follow-up	    Post-operative visit
    Exam	        Imaging or laboratory
    Meeting	        Team, supplier, or institutional
    Task	        Administrative reminder
    Block	        Vacation, congress, unavailability

### 9.4 Appointment Fields
    Field	                Type	                                                    Required
    Type	                Enum	                                                    Yes
    Title	                Text	                                                    Yes
    Patient	                Reference (patient_id)	                                    No
    Project	                Reference (project_id)	                                    No
    Procedure	            FK	                                                        No
    Start / End	            Timestamp	                                                Yes
    Location	            Text	                                                    No
    Status	                Enum (scheduled, confirmed, completed, cancelled, no_show)	Yes
    Notes	                Text	                                                    No

### 9.5 State Machine
text
scheduled ──▶ confirmed ──▶ completed
                        ├──▶ cancelled
                        └──▶ no_show
### 9.6 Actions
Create, edit, cancel, mark completed, navigate (day, week, month, today).

### 9.7 Future Extensions
iCal export and import.

Google Calendar and Outlook synchronization.

Desktop reminders.

Recurring appointments.

These are not part of the initial release.

## 10. Tab: Document Templates
### 10.1 Purpose
A list of pre-configured documents that can be applied to a patient in the Patient Module. Templates are reusable, translatable, and versioned independently of any project.

###10.2 Categories

    Category	    Built-in	Examples
    Orientations	Yes	        Post-operative care
    Contracts	    Yes	        Service agreement
    Consent terms	Yes	        Surgical consent
    Prescriptions	Yes	        Post-operative prescription
    Certificates	No	        Attendance certificate
    Declarations	No	        Procedure declaration

The first four are built-in and non-deletable. Others can be added by the user.

### 10.3 Template Fields
    Field	                Type	                            Required
    Kind	                Enum (category)	                    Yes
    Name (translation key)	Text	                            Yes
    File path	            Text (relative to templates/)	    Yes
    Created / Updated	    Timestamp	                        Auto

### 10.4 Format
Templates are plain text files (.txt) with placeholders using the syntax {{variable_name}}.

    Dear {{patient_name}},
    
    You underwent {{procedure_name}} on {{procedure_date}}.
    Please follow the post-operative instructions below.
    
    Sincerely,
    {{professional_salutation}} {{professional_name}}
    {{professional_registration_type}} {{professional_registration}}
    10.5 Placeholders
    Available variables include (non-exhaustive):
    
    Patient: patient_name, patient_social_name, patient_birth_date, patient_cpf.
    
    Procedure: procedure_name, procedure_date.
    
    Professional: professional_salutation, professional_name, professional_registration_type, professional_registration, clinic_name, clinic_address.
    
    System: today.

### 10.6 Actions
Create, import, edit, delete, and create a new category.

### 10.7 Use in the Patient Module
The Patient Module displays the list of templates and allows the user to apply one to a patient. Variables are filled from the patient record, the active procedure, and the professional profile. The rendered document is saved as a patient document.

This is the primary interface between the Professional Module and the Patient Module.

## 11. Internationalization
All specialties, categories, and procedures are always translated.

Built-in entities have translation keys in translations/<lang>/professional.json, provided by CranioZ.

Custom entities have translation keys of the form user.<kind>.<uuid>, provided by the user.

Custom translations live in ~/.cranioz/professional/translations/<lang>.json.

When a translation is missing in the active language, the fallback sequence is:

* The entity's source_language (the language at creation time).
* 
* The translation key itself.
* 
* A neutral placeholder.

The TranslationCatalogRegistry loads catalog sources in this precedence order (highest to lowest):

* Professional (user-created entities).
* 
* Settings (user overrides).
* 
* System (built-in).

12. Application Service
12.1 ProfessionalService
The ProfessionalService is the sole owner of the professional database. It exposes commands, reads the repository, and validates inputs.

Conceptual interface:

    class ProfessionalService(Protocol):
        # Profile
        def get_profile(self) -> ProfessionalProfile: ...
        def update_profile(self, command: UpdateProfessionalProfileCommand) -> None: ...
    
        # Catalog
        def list_specialties(self) -> list[Specialty]: ...
        def list_categories(self, specialty_id: str) -> list[Category]: ...
        def list_procedures(self, category_id: str | None = None) -> list[Procedure]: ...
        def create_procedure(self, command: CreateProcedureCommand) -> None: ...
        def update_procedure(self, command: UpdateProcedureCommand) -> None: ...
        def delete_procedure(self, command: DeleteProcedureCommand) -> None: ...
    
        # Templates
        def list_templates(self, kind: str) -> list[DocumentTemplate]: ...
        def create_template(self, command: CreateDocumentTemplateCommand) -> None: ...
        def update_template(self, command: UpdateDocumentTemplateCommand) -> None: ...
        def delete_template(self, command: DeleteDocumentTemplateCommand) -> None: ...
    
        # Schedule
        def list_appointments(self, start: date, end: date) -> list[Appointment]: ...
        def create_appointment(self, command: CreateAppointmentCommand) -> None: ...
        def update_appointment(self, command: UpdateAppointmentCommand) -> None: ...
        def cancel_appointment(self, command: CancelAppointmentCommand) -> None: ...
    
        # Budgets
        def list_budgets(self, patient_id: str | None = None) -> list[Budget]: ...
        def get_budget(self, budget_id: str) -> Budget: ...
        def create_budget(self, command: CreateBudgetCommand) -> None: ...
        def update_budget(self, command: UpdateBudgetCommand) -> None: ...
        def delete_budget(self, command: DeleteBudgetCommand) -> None: ...
    
        # Lifecycle
        def export_data(self) -> Path: ...
        def import_data(self, archive: Path) -> None: ...
        def backup(self) -> Path: ...

### 12.2 Repository
A ProfessionalRepository port abstracts SQLite access. The concrete adapter lives in infrastructure/persistence/professional/.

### 12.3 Ownership
The ProfessionalService is the only component that accesses the professional database. The onboarding wizard and the Professional Module both use the service; neither accesses the database directly.

## 13. Onboarding
### 13.1 First Run
On first boot, if no professional profile exists, an onboarding wizard collects the minimal fields:

* Salutation
* Full name
* Registration type
* Registration number

The wizard dispatches an UpdateProfessionalProfileCommand and closes. The remaining fields (email, address, logo, signature) are filled later via the Professional tab.

### 13.2 Skipping
The wizard can be skipped. In that case, generated documents will contain placeholders for the missing fields, and the user is warned.

### 13.3 Re-entry
There is no separate "onboarding" after the first run. All edits happen through the Professional tab.

## 14. Commands
The module introduces the following application Commands:

    Profile
    
    UpdateProfessionalProfileCommand
    
    Catalog
    
    CreateSpecialtyCommand, UpdateSpecialtyCommand, DeleteSpecialtyCommand
    
    CreateCategoryCommand, UpdateCategoryCommand, DeleteCategoryCommand
    
    CreateProcedureCommand, UpdateProcedureCommand, DeleteProcedureCommand
    
    Templates
    
    CreateDocumentTemplateCommand, UpdateDocumentTemplateCommand, DeleteDocumentTemplateCommand, ImportDocumentTemplateCommand
    
    Schedule
    
    CreateAppointmentCommand, UpdateAppointmentCommand, CancelAppointmentCommand, CompleteAppointmentCommand
    
    Budgets
    
    CreateBudgetCommand, UpdateBudgetCommand, DeleteBudgetCommand, ChangeBudgetStatusCommand, ExportBudgetPdfCommand
    
    Maintenance
    
    ExportProfessionalDataCommand
    
    ImportProfessionalDataCommand
    
    BackupProfessionalDataCommand

15. Interfaces with Other Modules
### 15.1 Patient Module
    Direction	            Data
    Professional → Patient	Document templates (applied to a patient)
    Professional → Patient	Budgets (created for a patient)
    Professional → Patient	Procedure catalog (referenced in patient history)
    Professional → Patient	Schedule (appointments linked to a patient)
    Patient → Professional	Patient identity (name, birth date, contact) for rendering templates and budgets

The interface is documented in docs/architecture/professional_patient_interface.md.

### 15.2 Project Module
The Professional Module references projects by project_id when a budget or appointment is linked to a project. It never reads or writes project files directly.

### 15.3 Osteotomy Module
Osteotomy patterns are not part of the Professional Module. They live in a separate shared catalog (see ADR-0006).

## 16. Security and Privacy
### 16.1 Sensitive Data
The professional database may contain patient-identifying information (names, contact details) in budgets and appointments. Although it does not hold clinical data, it falls under the same privacy considerations.

### 16.2 File Permissions
The ~/.cranioz/professional/ directory is created with permissions restricted to the operating system user.

### 16.3 Encryption
SQLCipher is available as an optional encryption layer. When enabled, the database and its backups are encrypted with a user-provided key. When disabled, the database is plaintext but access-restricted.

### 16.4 Audit
A professional_audit_log table records creation, modification, and deletion of budgets and appointments, including actor, timestamp, and affected entity. Audit logging is optional in the first release.

### 16.5 Export and Import
Exported archives contain patient-identifying information. The user is warned before exporting, and the archive may be encrypted.

## 17. Backup, Export, and Import
### 17.1 Automatic Backup
On application close (or at a configurable interval), the database is copied to professional.db.bak and to ~/.cranioz/backups/professional/. The last five backups are retained by default.

### 17.2 Manual Export
The user can export the entire professional database as a .cranioz-professional archive (a zip containing professional.db, templates/, attachments/, and a manifest.json with version metadata).

### 17.3 Import
Importing an archive replaces the current database after confirmation. Conflict resolution policies (merge, replace, rename) are defined in ADR-0009.

18. Out of Scope
The following are explicitly out of scope for the initial release:

* Multiple professionals on the same installation.
* 
* Integration with external ERP, billing, or insurance systems.
* 
* Advanced financial analytics and reports.
* 
* Cryptographic digital signature (ICP-Brasil).
* 
* Conditional and loop constructs in document templates.
* 
* Recurring appointments.
* 
* Bidirectional calendar synchronization.
* 
* These may be added in future versions if demand justifies them.

## 19. Related Documents
    docs/architecture/domain/professional/ — domain entities.
    
    docs/architecture/professional_patient_interface.md — interface with the Patient Module.
    
    docs/architecture/config/cranioz_home.md — structure of ~/.cranioz/.
    
    docs/architecture/internationalization/internationalization.md — translation system.
    
    docs/architecture/overview.md — overall architecture.

# Datatypes

## 1. Overview

A **Datatype** defines the fundamental structure of the data manipulated by CranioZ.

It answers the question:

> **How is this data represented?**

A Datatype does not, by itself, define the clinical meaning of the data.

For example, a triangular surface loaded from an STL file is a **Mesh**. It contains vertices, faces, normals, and other geometric information, but the `Mesh` Datatype does not know whether that surface represents a mandible, maxilla, tooth, osteosynthesis plate, or surgical guide.

This interpretation is assigned through a **Semantic Type**.

The architecture therefore separates:

```text
Object
├── Datatype
├── Semantic Type
├── Components
├── Capabilities
└── Relationships
```

Each concept has a specific responsibility:

```text
Datatype      → how the data is structured
Semantic      → what the data represents
Components    → what the object contains
Capabilities  → what the object can do
Relationships → how it relates to other objects
```

This separation allows Datatypes to remain generic and reusable, while clinical semantics determine how each Object is used by CranioZ.

---

# 2. Datatype × Semantic Type

A **Datatype** represents the structure of the data.

A **Semantic Type** represents the meaning assigned to that data.

For example:

```text
Mesh
└── Semantic Type: Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
```

or:

```text
Image2D
└── Semantic Type: Imaging.PanoramicRadiograph
```

or:

```text
Mesh
└── Semantic Type: Implant.OsteosynthesisPlate
```

Different Objects can therefore share the same Datatype:

```text
Mesh
├── Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
├── Anatomy.Bone.Maxilla
├── Anatomy.Tooth
├── Anatomy.Face
├── Implant.Dental
├── Implant.OsteosynthesisPlate
└── Surgical.SurgicalGuide
```

The Datatype remains `Mesh` in all of these cases.

What changes is the semantic classification assigned to the Object and, consequently, the Components and Capabilities that may be associated with or made available to it.

---

# 3. Objects and Datatypes

The Datatype is not the Object.

An Object is the entity manipulated by CranioZ and may use a Datatype as its underlying data representation.

Conceptually:

```text
Object
├── id
├── name
├── datatype
├── semantic_type
├── components
├── state
└── relationships
```

For example:

```text
Object
├── Datatype: Mesh
├── Semantic Type: Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
└── Components:
    └── Transform
```

In this case, `Mesh` defines the geometric structure, while `Mandible` defines the clinical meaning.

This distinction prevents the system from having to create specific classes for every possible combination.

Instead of:

```text
MandibleMesh
OrthognathicMandibleMesh
SegmentedMandibleMesh
MandibleWithLandmarks
MandibleWithImplantPlanning
```

the CranioZ architecture can represent these combinations through composition:

```text
Object
├── Datatype: Mesh
├── Semantic: Mandible
├── Components:
│   ├── Transform
│   └── Landmark
└── Capabilities:
    ├── Osteotomy
    ├── Landmark
    └── OrthognathicMovement
```

---

# 4. Fundamental Datatypes

The fundamental Datatypes represent generic data structures used throughout the system.

They should remain independent of clinical semantics whenever possible.

The main Datatypes currently planned are:

```text
Mesh
Volume
Image2D
Curve
Point
PointCloud
ROI
```

Additional Datatypes may be introduced as the system evolves.

---

# 5. Mesh

`Mesh` represents a three-dimensional polygonal geometry.

A Mesh may contain:

* vertices;
* edges;
* faces;
* normals;
* per-vertex attributes*



======================================================================
FILE: docs\architecture\objects\folder_structure.md
======================================================================

The architecture of Objects can be summarized as follows:

        domain/
        │
        ├── objects/
        │       Object
        │
        ├── datatypes/
        │       Mesh
        │       Volume
        │       Image2D
        │       Curve
        │       Point
        │       PointCloud
        │       ROI
        │
        ├── components/
        │       Transform
        │       Render
        │       Anatomical
        │       Landmark
        │       Image
        │       Volume
        │       Planning
        │
        ├── semantics/
        │       SemanticRegistry
        │       SemanticDefinition
        │
        ├── capabilities/
        │       CapabilityRegistry
        │       CapabilityResolver
        │       CapabilityDefinition
        │
        ├── relationships/
        │       RelationshipGraph
        │       Relationship
        │
        └── object_system/
                ObjectRegistry
                ObjectFactory
                ObjectRepository
                ObjectTypeRegistry





======================================================================
FILE: docs\architecture\objects\objects.md
======================================================================

## 1. The Conceptual Model
An Object can be understood through four complementary dimensions:


    Object
    │
    ├── Data
    │     └── What is the data?
    │
    ├── Semantic
    │     └── What does this data represent?
    │
    ├── Components
    │     └── What additional information/structures does it have?
    │
    └── Capabilities
          └── What can be done with this Object?
For example:


    Mandible
    │
    ├── Data
    │     └── Mesh
    │
    ├── Semantic
    │     └── Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
    │
    ├── Components
    │     ├── Transform
    │     ├── LandmarkSet
    │     └── AnatomicalProperties
    │
    └── Capabilities
          ├── Transform
          ├── Measure
          ├── Boolean
          ├── Cut
          ├── Osteotomy
          └── Landmark
This is much better than creating classes such as:


    MandibleMesh
    MandibleSurgicalMesh
    MandibleOrthognathicMesh
    MandibleSegmentedMesh
    MandibleOsteotomyMesh

## 2. Data: the physical representation
I would rename the previous term Data Type to simply Data or Data Representation.

The question is:

"What data does this Object contain or represent?"

Examples:


    Mesh
    Volume
    Image
    ImageSeries
    Point
    Curve
    Surface
    Region

This has no clinical semantics.

A Mesh can be:

    Mandible
    Maxilla
    Tooth
    Implant
    Plate
    Scan
    SurgicalGuide
Therefore:


    Mesh ≠ Mandible
and:

    Mandible ≠ Mesh

The mandible may have a Mesh as its geometric representation.

## 3. Semantic: the meaning of the Object
The second dimension is semantics.

The question is:

"What does this data represent?"

For example:

    Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
    Anatomy.Bone.Maxilla
    Anatomy.Tooth
    Anatomy.Zygoma
    
    Implant.Dental
    Implant.Facial
    
    Surgical.Osteotomy
    Surgical.Guide
    Surgical.Splint
    
    Imaging.CT
    Imaging.CBCT
    Imaging.Panoramic
This resolves an important issue:


    data:
        Mesh
    
    semantic:
        Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
The same Mesh could be:


    data:
        Mesh
    
    semantic:
        Scan.Intraoral
or:


    data:
        Mesh

    semantic:
        Implant.Facial
The geometry does not need to know these semantics.

## 4. Components
Here I would preserve an important part of the previous architecture, but make a distinction.

A Component represents additional information or structure associated with the Object.

Example:

    Mandible
    │
    ├── Mesh
    ├── Transform
    ├── LandmarkSet
    └── AnatomicalProperties
Or:

    PanoramicRadiograph
    │
    ├── Image
    ├── Transform2D
    └── ImageProperties
Or:


    Plate
    │
    ├── Mesh
    ├── Transform
    ├── PlateProperties
    └── MaterialProperties
This is different from Capability.

Component: "What does the Object have?"

Capability: "What can be done with the Object?"

This distinction is extremely useful.

## 5. Capabilities
Here is one of the most interesting ideas from the previous architecture, which I would keep.

A Capability represents an operational capability that can be applied to a given Object.

For example:


    Mandible
        capabilities:
            Transform
            Measure
            Boolean
            Cut
            Osteotomy
            Landmark
While:


    PanoramicRadiograph
        capabilities:
            Transform2D
            WindowLevel
            Crop
            Annotation
            Export
This avoids:

    if object.semantic_type == "Mandible":
        show_osteotomy_tool()

and enables something conceptually closer to:


    Object
        ↓
    CapabilityRegistry
        ↓
    Capabilities
        ↓
    Tools
The Toolbar, context, or module can then ask:

"Which Tools are compatible with the capabilities of this Object?"

## 6. But I would make an important change
I would not place capabilities as a static property simply stored on the Object.

That is, I would avoid thinking of:

python
object.capabilities = [
    "Transform",
    "Measure",
    "Osteotomy"
]
as being the definitive source of the architecture.

The Capability should be resolved by the system from the Object, its semantics, components, context, and, eventually, state.

For example:


    Mandible
        semantic:
            Anatomy.Bone.Mandible

components:

        Mesh
        Transform
        LandmarkSet

            ↓

    CapabilityResolver

            ↓

        Transform
        Measure
        Landmark
        Boolean
        Cut
        Osteotomy

This is more powerful because a Capability can depend on context.

For example:

    Mandible
may have:

    Osteotomy
but a particular osteotomy may require:

    Mesh
    + valid geometry
      + anatomical state

Therefore, a Capability is not just a "label". It can represent a verifiable capability of the system.

## 7. Capability is not Tool
This distinction is also important for the documentation we are writing now.

For example:

    Capability
        Osteotomy
means:

This Object can participate in osteotomy operations.

But the Tools may be:


    Create Osteotomy Plane
    Create BSSO
    Create Le Fort I
    Split Segment
    Move Segment
Therefore:


    Object
        ↓
    Capability
        ↓
    Tool
        ↓
    Command
        ↓
    Domain/Application Service
This chain becomes quite elegant for CranioZ.

## 8. The role of the ObjectRegistry
Here I would make a small correction relative to the previous architecture.

The ObjectRegistry should answer:

"Which Objects exist in this context?"

For example:

    ObjectRegistry
    │
    ├── Object #001
    │     semantic = Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
    │
    ├── Object #002
    │     semantic = Anatomy.Bone.Maxilla
    │
    ├── Object #003
    │     semantic = Anatomy.Tooth
    │
    └── Object #004
          semantic = Imaging.CBCT
It should not be responsible for determining the clinical meaning of the Object.

For that, there is the:

    SemanticRegistry
And to discover what can be done:


    CapabilityRegistry
Thus:


    ObjectRegistry
        "Which objects exist?"
    
    SemanticRegistry
        "What do these objects represent?"
    
    CapabilityRegistry
        "Which capabilities are available?"
    
    ObjectFactory
        "How are these objects created?"
This division remains very good.

## 9. ObjectTypeRegistry
I would also keep the ObjectTypeRegistry, but with a more precise definition.

It answers:

"Which types of Objects does the system know about?"

For example:

    ObjectTypeRegistry
    
        Mesh
        Volume
        Image
        ImageSeries
        Point
        Curve
        Landmark
        Measurement
        Transform
        CoordinateSystem
        Scan
        AnatomicalObject
        SurgicalGuide
        ...
This is different from the SemanticRegistry.

For example:

    Object Type:
        Mesh
    
    Semantic:
        Anatomy.Bone.Mandible

## 10. SceneGraph and RelationshipGraph
The previous architecture also had two structures worth preserving.

SceneGraph

It answers:

    "Where is this Object in the spatial/visual organization of the project?"

For example:

    Scene
    │
    ├── Patient
    │   ├── Maxilla
    │   ├── Mandible
    │   ├── Teeth
    │   └── FacialScan
    │
    └── Planning
        ├── Osteotomy
        ├── Segments
        └── SurgicalGuide
RelationshipGraph

It answers:

    "What is the semantic or dependency relationship between these Objects?"

For example:

    Measurement
        ├── references → Landmark A
        └── references → Landmark B
or:

    Segmentation
        └── derived-from → Volume
or:


    Mesh
        └── representation-of → Mandible
This separation is useful because visual hierarchy is not necessarily a domain relationship.

## 11. A more mature architecture
Combining the previous architecture with the current one, I would arrive at:


                              OBJECT
                                 │
              ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
              │                  │                   │
            DATA              SEMANTIC           COMPONENTS
              │                  │                   │
          Mesh                 Mandible           Transform
          Volume               Maxilla            LandmarkSet
          Image                Tooth              Properties
          Point                Implant
          Curve                Scan
          Region               ...
              │                  │
              └──────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                                 │
                                 ↓
                         CAPABILITY RESOLUTION
                                 │
                ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
                │                │                │
             Measure          Transform        Osteotomy
             Boolean          Landmark          Cut
             Register         Segment           ...
                │                │                │
                └────────────────┼────────────────┘
                                 ↓
                               TOOLS
                                 ↓
                              COMMANDS
                                 ↓
                         DOMAIN / APPLICATION
This preserves the original idea but prevents the Object from becoming a "god object".

## 12. Example: Mandible
I would document the example as follows:


    Object
    │
    ├── id
    │
    ├── data
    │   └── Mesh
    │
    ├── semantic
    │   └── Anatomy.Bone.Mandible
    │
    ├── components
    │   ├── Transform
    │   ├── LandmarkSet
    │   └── AnatomicalProperties
    │
    └── relationships
        ├── representation-of → Mandible
        └── references → ...
And its capabilities would be resolved:


    Mandible
        ↓
    CapabilityResolver
        ↓
    Transform
    Measure
    Boolean
    Cut
    Osteotomy
    Landmark
    Symmetry
We do not need to create:


    MandibleMesh
    MandibleSurgicalMesh
    MandibleOrthognathicMesh

## 13. Example: Panoramic Radiograph

    Object
    │
    ├── id
    │
    ├── data
    │   └── Image
    │
    ├── semantic
    │   └── Imaging.Radiography.Panoramic
    │
    ├── components
    │   ├── Transform2D
    │   └── ImageProperties
    │
    └── relationships
        └── ...
Capabilities:

    Transform2D
    WindowLevel
    Crop
    Annotation
    Measure2D
    Export
    ExternalEditor
    Note that the Object does not need to know that these capabilities correspond to buttons on a Toolbar.


======================================================================
FILE: docs\architecture\scene\scene.md
======================================================================

# Scene

## 1. Overview

A **Scene** represents the spatial organization and state of domain
objects within a specific planning context in CranioZ.

The Scene is a **domain-level concept**, independent of UI, rendering
engine, viewport implementation, and visualization framework.

The Scene defines:

- object membership;
- spatial hierarchy;
- spatial relationships;
- object transforms;
- reference frames;
- the coordinate system.

The Scene does **not** own the lifecycle of domain objects.

> **The Scene does not own domain objects. It owns the spatial
> organization of objects within a specific planning context.**

A domain object represents **what** an object is.
A Scene represents **where** it exists and **how** it relates to other
objects.
A rendering representation represents **how** it is visualized.
Domain Object ≠ Scene ≠ Rendering Actor ≠ Viewport

text

---

## 2. Architectural Role

The Scene belongs to the Domain layer. 

    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ UI │
    │ Areas · Editors · Toolbars · Overlays │
    └──────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
    │
    ┌──────────────────▼───────────────────────┐
    │ Application │
    │ Tools · Commands · Services · Flows │
    │ Transactions · Event Coordination │
    └──────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
    │
    ┌──────────────────▼───────────────────────┐
    │ Domain │
    │ Project │
    │ ├── DomainObjectStore │
    │ └── Scenes │
    │ ├── Membership │
    │ ├── Hierarchy │
    │ ├── Transforms │
    │ └── Relationships │
    └──────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
    │
    ┌──────────────────▼───────────────────────┐
    │ Infrastructure │
    │ DICOM · VTK · ITK · OCCT · Storage │
    │ Rendering Adapters · Spatial Indexes │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────┘


The UI layer is composed of Areas (structural containers), Editors (functional units hosted in Areas), Toolbars (internal to Editors, see ADR-0002), and Overlays (floating layers managed by the Workspace).
See ADR-0001 for the relationship between Area and Editor.

A **View** is a visual representation of an aspect of the Scene. It is rendered by a **Viewport**, hosted by an **Editor**, hosted by an **Area**. The Scene does not know about Viewports or Views.

---

## 3. Project and Scene Context

A Scene always belongs to a **Project**, which provides the persistent
boundary.

    Project
    ├── DomainObjectStore
    └── Scenes
    ├── Scene A
    ├── Scene B
    └── ...

The Project is responsible for the complete persistent planning context.
The Scene is responsible only for its spatial context.


## 4. DomainObjectStore

The **DomainObjectStore** is the canonical registry and lifecycle
authority for domain objects belonging to a Project.

    Project
    └── DomainObjectStore
    ├── Maxilla
    ├── Mandible
    ├── Tooth 11
    ├── Tooth 21
    ├── CT Volume
    └── Surgical Splint


The DomainObjectStore is **Project-scoped**, not application-global.

This provides:

- isolation between Projects;
- deterministic object identity;
- explicit lifecycle boundaries;
- straightforward persistence;
- prevention of cross-Project references.

The detailed contract is defined separately.

---

## 5. Scene and DomainObjectStore

The Scene references objects belonging to the DomainObjectStore of the
same Project.

    Project
    ├── DomainObjectStore
    │ ├── Maxilla
    │ ├── Mandible
    │ └── Splint
    │
    └── Scene
    ├── object_id → Maxilla
    ├── object_id → Mandible
    └── object_id → Splint


The Scene does not create canonical copies.

> **While an object is a member of a Scene, its object ID must resolve
> to a valid domain object in the Project's DomainObjectStore.**

This invariant must hold throughout the lifetime of the Scene.

---

## 6. Ownership vs. Membership

**Ownership** and **membership** are distinct concepts.

**Object ownership.** The DomainObjectStore owns the canonical lifecycle.
Import CT → Create Volume Object → Register in DomainObjectStore → Add to Scene


**Scene membership.** A domain object participates in the spatial context
of a Scene. Membership does not transfer ownership.

    Scene
    ├── Maxilla
    ├── Mandible
    └── Splint

The Scene maintains references through stable identities.

---

## 7. Membership Integrity

Every membership reference must resolve to a valid object in the
DomainObjectStore.
Scene Membership → DomainObjectStore.get(object_id) → Valid Domain Object


The Scene must never intentionally contain dangling references.

---

## 8. Object Removal vs. Membership Removal

**Remove membership.**
Scene.remove_object(object_id)



Removes spatial membership. The domain object remains in the
DomainObjectStore.

**Remove object from Project.**
DomainObjectStore.remove(object_id)


Removes the canonical domain object.

An object must not be removed from the DomainObjectStore while it is a
member of any Scene in the same Project.

    Remove Object from Project
    ↓
    Check Scene Memberships
    ↓
    ┌────┴────┐
    Exists None
    │ │
    ▼ ▼
    Reject Remove


The Application Core coordinates this operation.

---

## 9. Scene Core

The Scene Core contains the fundamental spatial state:

- Scene identity;
- metadata;
- object membership;
- coordinate-system definition;
- spatial hierarchy;
- transforms;
- spatial relationships;
- persistent reference-frame associations.

The Scene Core must remain deliberately small. It must not become a general-purpose container for Project state.

---

## 10. Spatial Hierarchy

Spatial hierarchy represents parent-child relationships that affect spatial interpretation.

    Mandible
    ├── Left Segment
    └── Right Segment
or:

    Patient Reference
    ├── Maxilla
    └── Mandible

Hierarchy is part of the Scene's spatial domain state.

---

## 11. Parent-Child Rules

An object may have **at most one parent within a given Scene**.

    Scene
    └── Mandible
    ├── Left Segment
    └── Right Segment


Conceptual operations:

    set_parent()
    get_parent()
    iter_children()


The hierarchy must not contain cycles.

The parent relationship belongs to the Scene, not to the domain object.
The same object may participate in different Scenes with different
parents.

    Scene A Scene B
    └── Mandible └── Osteotomy Reference
    └── Segment └── Segment

Only the spatial organization changes.

---

## 12. Transform Composition

    Parent-child hierarchy provides a basis for transform composition.
    Scene → Mandible → Left Segment

    T_scene_segment = T_scene_mandible · T_mandible_segment



For deeper hierarchies:

    T_scene_object = T_scene_parent · T_parent_child · T_child_object

The exact mathematical representation belongs to the geometry layer. Scene depends on domain-level transformation abstractions, not directly on NumPy, VTK, or OCCT.

---

## 13. Spatial Relationships

Not every relationship is parent-child hierarchy.

    SpatialRelationship
    ├── source
    ├── target
    ├── type
    ├── directed
    └── data


Examples:

    Registration(CT, IOS)
    AttachedTo(Plate, Mandible)
    DerivedFrom(OsteotomySegment, Mandible)


The Scene provides the spatial context. The algorithms that establish relationships belong to domain or application services.

---

## 14. Relationship Cardinality

A relationship type defines its own semantic constraints.

A relationship may be:

- directed or symmetric;
- unique or non-unique;
- one-to-one, one-to-many, many-to-many.

These properties must be defined by the relationship type.

Duplicate relationships should not be silently created.

---

## 15. Relationship Lifecycle

Relationships cannot reference objects that are not valid Scene members.

> **A Scene must not retain an active relationship whose required
> object references are no longer valid Scene members.**

Relationship cleanup is part of the Scene's structural integrity.

---

## 16. Coordinate System

Each Scene defines a global coordinate system.

- Units: **millimetres (mm)**.
- Handedness, axis orientation, and origin convention must be defined.

The exact anatomical convention is a project-wide geometry decision, defined separately in the geometry documentation and corresponding ADR.

---

## 17. Scene Origin and Reference Frames

The Scene origin represents the global technical reference frame.

It must not be confused with clinically meaningful anatomical reference
frames.

    Scene Coordinate System
    ├── Patient Reference Frame
    ├── Cranial Reference Frame
    ├── Maxillary Reference Frame
    └── Mandibular Reference Frame

A reference frame with persistent identity and clinical meaning may be a domain object. A transient mathematical frame may exist only as
temporary state.

---

## 18. Object Transforms

Objects may have local coordinate systems and transforms into Scene space.
Local Object Coordinates → Transform → Scene Coordinate System


The Scene maintains the resulting spatial state.

---

## 19. Rigid Transformations

Rigid transformations are first-class Scene operations.

Appropriate for:

- bone segments;
- dental models;
- implants;
- plates;
- splints;
- surgical guides;
- reference objects.

A rigid transform preserves distances, angles, and topology.

The Scene uses a validated domain-level rigid transformation type.

---

## 20. Deformable Transformations

Deformable transformations are separate from rigid Scene transforms.
    
    Base Geometry
    ├── Rigid Transform
    └── Deformation Field



A deformation field may be represented by displacement fields,
deformation fields, morph targets, facial rigs, or other domain-specific
models.

The deformation representation belongs to the domain object, not to the
Scene.

---

## 21. Registration

Registration establishes spatial correspondence between coordinate
systems.

    CT
    │ T_ct_to_scene
    ▼
    Scene
    ▲ T_ios_to_scene
    │
    IOS

Registration algorithms belong to domain services, application services, or infrastructure-backed algorithms.

The Scene stores the resulting transformation and/or spatial relationship.

---

## 22. Derived Objects

Some objects are derived from others.

    CT → Segmentation → Bone Mesh
    
    Mandible → Osteotomy → Osteotomy Segment
    
    CT + Facial Scan → Registration → Facial Model


Derived relationships support reproducibility, dependency tracking, invalidation, regeneration, and persistence.

The application/module layer performs the operation. The Scene maintains spatial organization and relevant relationships.

---

## 23. Measurements

Measurements are interpreted within the Scene coordinate system unless
associated with another reference frame.

    Distance(PointA, PointB) → mm


Measurement algorithms remain separate domain services.

---

## 24. Rendering Independence

The Scene is completely independent of the rendering engine.

It must not contain `vtkActor`, `vtkRenderer`, `vtkMapper`, cameras,
lights, OpenGL state, or viewport objects.

The **Editor** consumes Scene state via `SceneQuery` (read-only). The
**Rendering Adapter** creates visual representations (Views) inside
Viewports.

    Scene → Editor (SceneQuery) → Rendering Adapter → Viewport → View


The Scene does not know about Viewports, Views, or Editors.

---

## 25. Rendering Representation

A domain object and its visual representation are separate entities.

    Mandible (domain object)
    │
    ├── Viewport3DEditor
    │ └── Viewport → View: surface representation
    │
    ├── MPRViewerEditor
    │ └── Viewport → View: contour/intersection
    │
    └── PlanningEditor
    └── Viewport → View: translucent surface

The same domain object may have different Views in different Viewports,
hosted by different Editors.

---

## 26. Rendering Adapter

The Rendering Adapter bridges domain/application state and rendering
infrastructure. A VTK implementation may contain:

- `VTKActorFactory` — converts domain representations into VTK objects.
- `ActorRegistry` — maps object IDs to rendering actors.
- `VTKSceneRenderer` — manages the VTK rendering context.
- `SceneBridge` — synchronizes Scene events with rendering representations.

These components belong to Infrastructure/UI integration, not the Domain
layer. The Scene remains unaware of them.

---

## 27. Presentation State

Presentation concerns include color, opacity, visibility, selection,
representation mode, shading, and rendering style.

These are not Scene properties. The same Scene object may have different
presentation states in different Viewports.

---

## 28. Membership vs. Visibility
Scene membership ≠ Viewport visibility

text

An object can remain a Scene member while hidden in one or more
Viewports. This supports multiple Areas and Viewports without modifying
domain state.

---

## 29. Selection

Selection is an Application/UI concern. The Scene does not know which
object is currently selected. Selection state remains outside the Scene
Core.

---

## 30. Temporary and Preview Objects

Temporary objects include cutting planes, boolean operands, collision
proxies, registration markers, preview osteotomies, temporary splints,
simulation results, and construction geometry.

Temporary objects should not automatically become persistent Project
objects. Only objects intended to become part of the persistent domain
model are registered in the DomainObjectStore.

---

## 31. Scene API

Conceptual operations:

**Core:**

    add_object()
    remove_object()
    contains()
    get_object()
    iter_objects()
    get_transform()
    set_transform()


**Graph:**

    set_parent()
    get_parent()
    iter_children()
    iter_subtree()


**Relationship:**

    add_relationship()
    remove_relationship()
    get_relationships()
    iter_relationships()

The API must remain independent of rendering frameworks.

---

## 32. Scene Invariants

- **Unique identity** — object identity is unique within the Project's
  DomainObjectStore.
- **Valid membership** — every membership reference resolves to a valid
  object.
- **No dangling membership** — the Scene never retains membership for a
  removed object.
- **Valid hierarchy** — no cycles; at most one parent per object per Scene.
- **Valid relationships** — required endpoints resolve to valid objects.
- **Valid transforms** — mathematically valid and using the Scene's
  coordinate conventions.
- **Consistent units** — mm.

---

## 33. Commands and Scene Mutations

Scene mutations should occur through Application Commands.

Examples: `AddObject`, `RemoveObjectFromScene`, `RemoveObjectFromProject`,
`SetParent`, `TransformObject`, `AddRelationship`, `RemoveRelationship`,
`ApplyRigidMovement`.
Tool → Command → Application Service → DomainObjectStore / Scene → Events

text

This provides validation, transactions, undo/redo, event publication,
and auditability.

---

## 34. Transactions

Complex planning operations may modify multiple objects and Scene state.

    Apply Le Fort I Movement


may affect the maxillary segment, dental model, cephalometric landmarks,
and surgical splint.

Such operations are coordinated by the Application Core as a single logical transaction.

The Scene does not own transaction orchestration. It provides a state model that allows the Application Core to capture and restore the
relevant state.

---

## 35. Transaction and Computation Snapshots

**Transaction snapshot** — optimized for rollback. Contains only the state necessary to restore the affected logical Scene state: membership, hierarchy, transforms, relationships, relevant domain state.

Large geometry payloads should not be unnecessarily duplicated. If a transaction modifies object content (e.g., a recalculated mesh), the snapshot must include enough information to restore it. Immutable
payloads may be shared by reference.

**Computation snapshot** — provides stable input to asynchronous processing. Contains geometry, volume data, landmarks, transforms, and relevant metadata.

Requirements: stable read access, immutability, safe use by worker threads, efficient transfer or sharing.

The two snapshot mechanisms may share infrastructure but have different contracts.

---

## 36. Transaction Rollback Example

    Apply Le Fort I Movement
    ↓
    Transaction Started
    ↓
    Transform Maxillary Segment
    ↓
    Update dependent spatial state
    ↓
    Generate / Update Splint
    ↓
    Validate Splint
    ↓
    Validation Failed
    ↓
    Transaction Rolled Back
    ↓
    Restore Previous State


The rendering layer receives the resulting committed state.

---

## 37. Scene Events

Meaningful Scene state changes generate semantic events:

    ObjectAdded
    ObjectRemovedFromScene
    ObjectTransformed
    ParentChanged
    RelationshipAdded
    RelationshipRemoved


Events describe semantic state changes, not UI interactions. `ObjectTransformed` is a domain event. `MouseDragged` is not.

A generic `SceneChanged` event is intentionally avoided.

---

## 38. Event Semantics

**State information.** A state-change event provides sufficient
information for observers to determine what changed.

    ObjectTransformed
    ├── object_id
    ├── previous_transform
    └── new_transform


**Transaction boundaries.** Events generated during a transaction are
not interpreted as committed state until the transaction commits.

    TransactionStarted
    TransactionCommitted
    TransactionRolledBack

**Deterministic ordering.** Within a committed transaction, event
ordering is deterministic. Observers are independent. No observer may
depend on another observer having processed an event first.

If one operation genuinely depends on another, that dependency must be
expressed explicitly in the Application layer.

---

## 39. Spatial Indexing

The Scene does not directly manage spatial indexing or rendering performance. It supports external spatial services: spatial indexes, bounding-volume hierarchies, collision detection, nearest-neighbor
queries, proximity queries, intersection queries, region-of-interest queries.

    Scene
    ├── ObjectAdded
    ├── ObjectRemoved
    └── ObjectTransformed
    ↓
    SpatialIndexService
    ↓
    Spatial Index



The Scene remains independent of the concrete indexing implementation.

---

## 40. Spatial Observers

Infrastructure and Application services may observe Scene events:
`SpatialIndexService`, `RenderingAdapter`, `MeasurementCache`,
`CollisionService`, `DependencyTracker`.

Observers maintain derived state. They do not acquire ownership.

Authoritative state remains in `DomainObjectStore + Scene`.

---

## 41. Concurrency and Threading

The Scene is **application-thread confined by default**.

Domain mutations occur through the Application Core on the designated application thread. Worker threads must not mutate the live Scene.

Long-running operations (segmentation, registration, mesh processing, collision computation, deformation simulation) operate on stable computation snapshots.
    
    Application Thread → Scene → Computation Snapshot → Worker Thread
    ↓
    Processing
    ↓
    Result / Command
    ↓
    Application Thread ← Scene ←──── commit ──────────────────┘


When concurrent access is required, immutable snapshots or explicitly
defined read models should be used.

---

## 42. Serialization

Scene state is serialized as part of the Project format.

Preserved:

- Scene identity;
- metadata;
- coordinate-system definition;
- object membership;
- hierarchy;
- transforms;
- spatial relationships;
- persistent reference-frame associations;
- persistent derived-object relationships.

Large binary assets are managed separately.

    Scene Data Assets
    ├── Object IDs      ├── DICOM
    ├── Hierarchy       ├── STL
    ├── Transforms      ├── OBJ
    ├── Relationships   └── other binary data
    └── Asset References

---

## 43. Serialization References

Scene deserialization resolves references between the Project's
DomainObjectStore and its Scenes.

The deserializer must reject or explicitly handle:

- missing object references;
- invalid hierarchy references;
- invalid relationship endpoints;
- unsupported schema versions;
- missing required assets.

For backward compatibility, the deserializer may apply schema migrations
before resolving references. Migration policies belong to the Project
serialization specification.

---

## 44. Multiple Scenes

A Project may contain multiple Scenes.

    Project
    ├── DomainObjectStore
    └── Scenes
    ├── Initial State
    ├── Surgical Plan
    ├── Simulation
    └── Comparison



Different Scenes may contain different memberships, hierarchies, transforms, and relationships while referencing the same objects.

For example, a Mandible may be a member of both an "Initial State" Scene and a "Surgical Plan" Scene, with different transforms in each. The DomainObjectStore owns the canonical Mandible; each Scene owns its own spatial organization of that Mandible.

The concept of an **active Scene** is application/UI state.

---

## 45. Scene and Flows

A Flow may operate on one or more Scenes.

    Orthognathic Flow
    ├── Tomography
    ├── Models
    ├── Cephalometry
    ├── Registration
    ├── Osteotomy
    └── Splint



The Flow orchestrates operations. The Scene provides the spatial state.
The Scene does not know which Flow is executing.

---

## 46. Scene and Modules

Modules create, modify, or consume Scene state through Application Commands and Services.

    Segmentation Module → Create Bone Object → DomainObjectStore → Add to Scene

    Osteotomy Module → Create Osteotomy Segment → DomainObjectStore → Add to Scene Hierarchy
    → Apply Transform


Modules must not bypass the Domain/Application boundaries by directly
manipulating rendering actors.

---

## 47. Example: Orthognathic Planning

    Scene (membership references to the DomainObjectStore)
    │
    ├── CT Volume
    ├── Maxilla
    ├── Mandible
    ├── Upper Dental Model
    ├── Lower Dental Model
    ├── Cephalometric Landmarks
    ├── Osteotomy Segments
    ├── Surgical Splint
    └── Reference Geometry


The Scene members are references to objects in the DomainObjectStore. The Scene does not duplicate them; it organizes them spatially.

    DomainObjectStore Scene
    ├── Mandible └── Mandible
    ├── Left Segment ├── Left Segment
    ├── Right Segment └── Right Segment
    └── Splint

---

## 48. Example: Composed Segment Movement
    
    Scene → Mandible
    ├── Left Segment
    └── Right Segment

    T_scene_left_segment = T_scene_mandible · T_mandible_left_segment


After surgical simulation:

    Left Segment → Transform = T_left
    Right Segment → Transform = T_right

The planning service calculates the transforms. The Scene maintains the resulting configuration. The rendering adapter observes the committed
state.

---

## 49. Example: Registration

    CT
    │ T_ct_to_scene
    ▼
    Scene
    ▲ T_ios_to_scene
    │
    IOS

The registration service calculates the transformation. The Scene stores the resulting spatial relationship. The rendering system displays both datasets in the same spatial context, without either domain object becoming dependent on VTK.

---

## 50. Example: Multiple Views

The same Scene may be consumed by multiple Editors, each hosting one or
more Viewports that render different Views of the Scene.

    Scene (spatial state)
    │
    ├── Viewport3DEditor
    │ └── Viewport → View: 3D representation
    │
    ├── MPRViewerEditor
    │ ├── Viewport → View: Axial slice
    │ ├── Viewport → View: Sagittal slice
    │ └── Viewport → View: Coronal slice
    │
    └── CurvedMPREditor
    └── Viewport → View: Panoramic reconstruction

Each Viewport may have its own camera, projection, orientation, visibility, representation mode, and selection.

These are not properties of the Scene Core.

All Viewports derive spatial information from the same Scene state. The Scene does not know which Viewports exist.

---

## 51. Example: Rendering Synchronization

**When an object is added:**

    AddObject
    ↓
    DomainObjectStore
    ↓
    Scene.add_object()
    ↓
    ObjectAdded
    ↓
    SceneBridge
    ↓
    VTKActorFactory → ActorRegistry → VTKSceneRenderer


**When an object is transformed:**

    TransformObject
    ↓
    Scene.set_transform()
    ↓
    ObjectTransformed
    ↓
    SceneBridge → Update vtkActor transform


The rendering system reacts to committed state.

---

## 52. Example: Spatial Index Synchronization

    ObjectAdded
    ↓ SpatialIndexService → Insert Object
    
    ObjectTransformed
    ↓ SpatialIndexService → Update Bounds
    
    ObjectRemovedFromScene
    ↓ SpatialIndexService → Remove Object


The Scene remains unaware of the concrete spatial-index implementation.

---

## 53. Reproducibility

The Scene should be deterministic enough to reconstruct the logical spatial planning state from serialized Project data.

Given `Project Data + Scene Data + Referenced Assets`, CranioZ should reconstruct the same logical spatial configuration.

This supports research reproducibility, planning review, collaboration, version control, automated testing, auditability, and Project migration.

---

## 54. What Scene Must Not Do

The Scene must not be responsible for:

- rendering;
- VTK actors, cameras, lights;
- layout (Area Layout Tree or Editor Layout Tree);
- UI selection;
- dock behavior;
- toolbar state (see ADR-0002);
- mouse interaction, keyboard shortcuts;
- DICOM or STL/OBJ loading;
- mesh processing algorithms;
- segmentation algorithms;
- registration algorithms;
- cephalometric calculations;
- surgical planning algorithms;
- persistence infrastructure;
- plugin discovery;
- module lifecycle management;
- rendering actor management;
- global Project object lifecycle.

These responsibilities belong to other architectural layers:

- **Editors** own their Toolbars and Toolbar state (ADR-0002).
- **Areas** own their Editors and their internal Editor Layout Tree
  (ADR-0001).
- **Workspace** owns the Area Layout Tree and Overlays.
- **Application Core** owns Commands, Transactions, and Services.
- **Infrastructure** owns Rendering Adapters, DICOM loading, and
  Spatial Indexes.

---

## 55. Architectural Summary

The Scene is the domain-level spatial context of CranioZ.
Spatial Membership + Spatial Hierarchy + Transforms

Spatial Relationships + Coordinate System + Reference Frames

Spatial State

text

The Project provides the persistent boundary.

The DomainObjectStore provides canonical object identity and lifecycle.

The Application layer provides Commands, Transactions, Services, Flows,
and Event Coordination.

Infrastructure implements Application ports (RenderingPort,
PersistencePort, ExternalComputationPort). The Scene does not depend on
Infrastructure implementations.
    
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ Project                                  │
    │ ┌──────────────────┐ ┌─────────────────┐ │
    │ │ DomainObjectStore│ │ Scenes          │ │
    │ │ Canonical Objects│ │ Membership      │ │
    │ │ Identity         │ │ Hierarchy       │ │
    │ │ Lifecycle        │ │ Transforms      │ │
    │ │                    │ │ Relationships │ │
    │ └────────┬─────────┘ └────────┬────────┘ │
    └───────────┼─────────────────────┼────────┘
    │            │
    └──────────┬──────────┘
    │
    Commands / Events / Transactions
    │
    ▼
    ┌─────────────────────┐
    │ Application Core    │
    │ Commands            │
    │ Transactions        │
    │ Services            │
    │ Flows               │
    └──────────┬──────────┘
                │
                ▼
    ┌─────────────────────┐
    │ Infrastructure      │
    │ Rendering Adapter   │
    │ Spatial Index       │
    │ Persistence         │
    └─────────────────────┘

The fundamental separation:

    DomainObjectStore → owns canonical domain object lifecycle
    Scene → owns spatial organization within a planning context
    Application Core → owns orchestration, commands, and transactions
    Rendering Adapter → translates domain state into visual representations
    Viewport → presents a View of the Scene
    Editor → hosts Viewports and Toolbars
    Area → hosts Editors
    Workspace → hosts Areas and Overlays

This separation provides CranioZ with a modular, multi-Scene, rendering-independent spatial architecture suitable for clinical planning, simulation, visualization, and future extensions.
