FILE: docs\architecture\ui\tools\tools.md
======================================================================

# Tools
## 1. Overview
A Tool represents an action or interaction mode made available to the user by a CranioZ module. Tools are one of the main interfaces between the user and the application's executable functionality, and may be presented in toolbars, menus, panels, contextual menus, or Editors without their definition depending on a specific Workspace location.

A Tool does not represent the module itself and should not contain clinical, geometric, or infrastructure logic. Its responsibility is to define what it represents, how it is identified, in which contexts it is available, and how interaction is initiated. Execution is delegated to the application layer through Commands and Application Services.

    User → Tool → Command → Command Executor → Application Service → Domain
Not every Tool results directly in a single Command. Interactive Tools may produce a sequence of operations or generate a Command only when interaction completes.

## 2. Objectives
The Tool system should provide a uniform interface for actions and interaction modes, allow modules and plugins to expose functionality, separate interaction and presentation from application logic, support multiple presentation forms for the same Tool, facilitate discovery and registration, support contextual availability, provide metadata for UI construction, allow activation by humans or automation, keep Tools independent of Workspace location, allow reuse by menus, shortcuts, workflows, scripts, and MCP, and enable extensibility through plugins.

A Tool should be sufficiently declarative for the application to determine what it is, when it can be used, and how it should be presented, without its implementation needing to know Workspace-specific details.

## 3. Tool, Command, and Service
A Tool represents an action or interaction mode made available to the user. It answers: what does it represent, how is it identified, how does it appear, in which contexts is it available, how is interaction initiated, what type of interaction does it support.

A Command represents an operation executable by the application. It answers: what operation will be executed, what are its parameters, how can it be undone, how does it participate in history, what changes does it produce, what events are generated.

A Service implements application operations or rules that should not belong to the Tool. Algorithms and specific rules remain in their appropriate layers.

    Tool      → may create/execute → Command
    Command   → does not necessarily depend on a Tool
    Service   → coordinates application logic
A Command may be triggered by a Tool, shortcut, menu, macro, workflow, automation, MCP, script, or internal operation. A Tool may represent a complex interaction resulting in one or more Commands. The preferred flow is:


    Tool → Command → CommandExecutor → CommandStack → Application Service → Domain

The CommandExecutor handles execution, history, and Undo/Redo. The Tool should not implement these responsibilities. CommandStack remains independent of the Tool.

Example:

    Tool:                Create Le Fort I Osteotomy
    Command:             CreateOsteotomyCommand
    Application Service: OsteotomyService
    Domain:              Osteotomy, Bone, Geometry

## 4. Tool as a Contract
Tool should be treated as a contract. Using Protocol (structural typing) rather than ABC reduces coupling and allows plugins to implement Tools without inheriting from a framework base class.

The base contract should remain small and stable, describing identity, metadata, presentation, and contextual availability. Execution is not mandatory in the base contract, since different Tool modalities exist.

    python
    class Tool(Protocol):
        identifier: str
        label: str
        description: str
        icon: str | None
        category: str | None
    
        def is_available(self, context: ToolContext) -> bool:
            ...

is_enabled does not belong exclusively to subclasses. The UI needs to present Tools of different modalities and determine whether each can be triggered. If the operation depended on inspecting the concrete modality to discover is_enabled, complexity would shift into UI components. Instead, the framework should resolve availability and enablement through a dedicated component (e.g., ToolStateResolver), using declared requirements and context. The UI must not rely on improvised type checks.

### 4.1 Action Tool
An ActionTool represents a discrete action. It normally results in the execution of a Command or application operation.

    python
    class ActionTool(Tool, Protocol):
        def execute(self, context: ToolContext) -> ToolResult:
        ...
Examples: Save, Undo, Add Landmark, Create Plate, Calculate Cephalometry.

### 4.2 Interactive Tool
An InteractiveTool represents a continuous interaction mode. The definitive interface should remain small.

    python
    class InteractiveTool(Tool, Protocol):
        def activate(self, context: ToolContext) -> None:
            ...
    
        def deactivate(self, context: ToolContext) -> None:
            ...
    
        def handle_event(self, event: ToolEvent, context: ToolContext) -> None:
        ...
Examples: Select, Move, Rotate, Measure, Place Landmark, Define Osteotomy Plane, Paint Segmentation, Sculpt.

## 5. Identity
Each Tool must have a unique and stable identifier.


    orthognathic.cephalometry.add_landmark
    osteotomy.create_lefort_i
The identifier should be unique within the system, remain stable across versions whenever possible, not depend on user-facing text, not depend on interface position, and be usable by menus, plugins, workflows, and automations. The label is intended for presentation only.

    yaml
    identifier: orthognathic.cephalometry.add_landmark
    label: Add Landmark
Changing the user-facing text should not change the Tool's identity.

## 6. Presentation and Metadata
A Tool should not determine where it is displayed. Positioning belongs to the UI layer and to the Workspace/Layout system.

    Tool                        → defines the action or interaction
    Workspace / Layout / Editor → defines where the Tool is presented

The Tool declares identity metadata: identifier, name, description, icon, semantic category, context requirements, and interaction modality. The UI configuration determines toolbar, menu position, display order, visual grouping, keyboard shortcut, and region-specific visibility.

category is a semantic metadata of the Tool. group deserves caution: if it does not have a consistent meaning for discovery or semantic grouping, it risks becoming merely a UI hint disguised as metadata. It should be kept only when there is a clear discovery or grouping use case, and treated as presentation metadata otherwise.

## 7. Tool and Editor
An Editor provides a specialized interaction space. A Tool provides an action or interaction mode usable in that space.

    3D Editor
    ├── Select, Move, Rotate, Measure, Landmark
    
    Cephalometric Editor
    ├── Add Landmark, Move Landmark, Remove Landmark, Measure Angle, Measure Distance
8. 
The Editor should not reimplement the logic of each Tool, and a Tool should not assume exclusive use by a particular Editor unless its definition explicitly requires a specific context.


    Editor → hosts / uses → Tool
and not:

    Tool → structurally belongs to → Editor

## 8. Execution Context
Tools often depend on the current state of the system. An osteotomy Tool may be available only when a patient is loaded, a valid mandible or maxilla exists, the model is in a compatible state, the module is available, a valid selection exists, and the current Editor supports the interaction.

ToolContext should represent the execution context provided by the framework, while services and specific interfaces provide access to the necessary operations. It should not become an excessively broad global context; each new need should not encourage additional dependencies in a monolithic context. Contexts may be segregated so that a Tool accesses only what it needs.


    class ToolContext(Protocol):
        @property
        def scene(self) -> SceneContext: ...
        @property
        def selection(self) -> SelectionContext: ...
        @property
        def editor(self) -> EditorContext | None: ...
        @property
        def permissions(self) -> PermissionContext: ...

A measurement tool may need selection and the active Editor, but not necessarily the Patient object or administrative permissions. The context may inform permissions, but effective authorization must be verified by the layer responsible for executing the operation.

### 8.1 Availability and Enablement
Available: the Tool makes sense in the current context and may be presented. Enabled: the Tool is available and can be activated immediately.

    Create Le Fort I Osteotomy

    Available: yes
    Enabled:   no

The Tool may be available because the osteotomy module is active and a patient exists, but remain disabled because no valid maxilla has been selected. The UI uses is_available(context) to decide whether to present the Tool and is_enabled(context) to decide whether it can be activated. This distinction prevents contextual logic from being embedded in UI components.

9. Tool State
The existence, availability, and activation of a Tool are different states. They are related properties, not necessarily successive phases of a universal state machine.

Concept	                Meaning
Registered	            The Tool was registered in the system.
Available	            The Tool can be used in the current context and presented.
Enabled	                The Tool can be triggered at that moment.
Active              	The Tool is executing an interaction session, when applicable.
Completed	            The interaction ended.

An ActionTool does not normally need a persistent Active state. An InteractiveTool may remain active until the user completes, cancels, or interrupts the interaction. Contextual state should be determined by the framework and the current context, not maintained as arbitrary state inside UI components. Tool state and session state are distinct: a Tool may be Available and Enabled with no active session, and the session has its own cycle (Idle → Interacting → Completed).

## 10. Execution and Result
Tool execution should not concentrate clinical, geometric, or infrastructure logic. The Tool acts as an interaction layer.

### 10.1 ToolResult
ToolResult and the result of a Command are distinct concepts. ToolResult is the result of the interaction or triggering of the Tool; the operational result belongs to the Command or service called.

An interactive Tool may complete successfully even when the operation requested by the user is cancelled before producing a persistent change. Likewise, a Tool may start an asynchronous Command and end its interaction before processing finishes.

    class ToolResult:
        interaction_status: Literal["completed", "cancelled", "interrupted"]
        command_result: CommandResult | None
        message: str | None
Keeping the results separate prevents ToolResult from becoming a generic container for all data produced by the system.

### 10.2 Threading and Asynchrony
Interactive Tools typically run on the UI thread, since they process input events and update previews. However, not every Command must be asynchronous. A simple command, such as changing a landmark property, may run synchronously. Segmentation, volume registration, complex geometric operations, and long calculations should be dispatched asynchronously when appropriate.

Recommended policy:

* Tool interaction: managed in the thread context required by the UI.
* Lightweight Commands: may run synchronously.
* Long operations: executed asynchronously by an appropriate mechanism.
* Shared state changes: coordinated by explicit concurrency and consistency rules.
* Preview and visual updates: synchronized with the UI thread.
* Cancellation: handled explicitly by the operation and the session lifecycle.

The Command — not the Tool — should declare its execution mode, so the CommandExecutor decides how to dispatch and the Tool remains unaware of threading.


    class Command(Protocol):
        execution_mode: Literal["sync", "async"] = "sync"

## 11. Interactive Tools
Some Tools represent continuous interaction with the user: Select, Move, Rotate, Draw, Measure, Place Landmark, Define Osteotomy Plane, Paint Segmentation, Sculpt, Place Implant. In these cases, the Tool has its own lifecycle.


    Idle → Activated → Interacting → Completed → Deactivated
Example:

    Define Osteotomy Plane
            ↓
    Tool activated
            ↓
    User selects points
            ↓
    Plane preview
            ↓
    User confirms
            ↓
    CreateOsteotomyCommand
            ↓
    Command executed
            ↓
    Tool deactivated
The Tool may function as an interaction state machine, while persistent changes remain the responsibility of the application/domain layer.

### 11.1 Tool Session
It is useful to distinguish the Tool definition from a specific interaction session.


    Tool        → tool definition
    ToolSession → state of a specific interaction

    class ToolSession:
        tool: Tool
        state: ToolState
        context: ToolContext
        started_at: datetime
        finished_at: datetime | None
This prevents the base Tool contract from being polluted with temporary interaction state. A session may exist only while the Tool is active.

### 11.2 Activation and Deactivation
Activation of interactive Tools should be managed by a dedicated component, such as ToolManager.

    python
    class ToolManager:
        def activate(self, identifier: str, context: ToolContext) -> None: ...
        def deactivate(self) -> None: ...
        def active_tool(self) -> Tool | None: ...

ToolManager may ensure that only one interactive Tool is active per Editor, the previous Tool is correctly deactivated, changing Editors ends the appropriate session, changing modules is handled correctly, activation and deactivation events are propagated, an ActionTool may be executed even while an InteractiveTool is active in the same Editor (provided there is no conflict), and switching Editors with an active session suspends or terminates the session according to a defined policy. Different Editors may have different active Tools, similarly to the editor model used by Blender.

12. Tool Modalities
Tools may be classified according to behavior. This classification is primarily behavioral and should not create an excessively complex class hierarchy.

Modality                        Description          	                                    Contract
Action Tool	                    Executes a discrete action.	                                ActionTool
Interactive Tool	            Remains active while the user interacts with the scene.	    InteractiveTool
Toggle Tool	                    Switches between two states.	                            ActionTool (flips state)
Stateful Tool	                Maintains a more complex interaction state.	                InteractiveTool (with state)

Examples: Save, Undo, Add Landmark, Create Plate, Calculate Cephalometry (Action); Select, Move, Measure, Place Landmark, Draw Osteotomy (Interactive); Snap, Grid, Visibility, Orthographic/Perspective (Toggle); Segmentation Brush, Sculpt, Registration, Osteotomy Planning (Stateful).

A ToggleTool may be modeled as an ActionTool whose execution flips a state and returns the new state in ToolResult. A StatefulTool may be modeled as an InteractiveTool whose session carries additional state. A dedicated contract should only be introduced if the existing ones prove insufficient. ActionTool and InteractiveTool define interaction modalities; Toggle and Stateful describe behaviors or characteristics, not classes. Creating a class for each combination would increase complexity without improving the contract.

## 13. Tool Groups and Categories
Tools may be grouped semantically for presentation and discovery.


    Cephalometry
    ├── Add Landmark, Move Landmark, Delete Landmark, Measure Distance, Measure Angle
    
    Osteotomy
    ├── Create Osteotomy, Edit Osteotomy, Move Segment, Rotate Segment, Confirm Osteotomy
    Grouping does not change the identity of the Tool.

Category is a broader semantic classification. Group is a contextual grouping of Tools for presentation.

    Category: Measurement
    Group:    Cephalometry
    Tool:     Measure Angle
Category defines the type of Tool; Group defines how it may be grouped in the UI. This prevents category from becoming a generic field responsible for different functions.

Initial categories may include: Selection, Navigation, Measurement, Annotation, Modeling, Planning, Segmentation, Analysis, Visualization, Documentation. Categories should not be excessively rigid; the framework may provide common categories, while modules and plugins may introduce specific ones when necessary.

## 14. Tool Parameters
Some Tools require parameters.


    Create Plate
    
    thickness     = 1.5 mm
    hole_diameter = 2.0 mm
    locking       = true
The Tool should not necessarily store these values as permanent state. When appropriate, it may request the parameters from the user and produce a parameterized Command.

    Create Plate Tool → Plate Configuration → CreatePlateCommand(thickness=1.5, hole_diameter=2.0, locking=True) → CommandExecutor
Recommended distinction:

The Tool defines or requests which parameters are required for the interaction.

The UI collects the concrete values.

The Command receives the concrete values required to execute the operation.

The Domain validates model-specific rules.

    Tool → Parameter Specification → UI → Concrete Parameters → Command → Domain
This keeps the definition of the operation separate from the presentation of its parameters.

15. Tool Registry and Discovery
CranioZ should provide a Tool registration mechanism.


    ToolRegistry
    ├── register(tool)
    ├── unregister(identifier)
    ├── get(identifier)
    ├── find(...)
    └── list(...)
The Registry allows modules to register Tools, plugins to add Tools, the UI to discover available Tools, menus and toolbars to be built dynamically, automation systems to find Tools, and Tools to be queried by identifier.


    Module → register Tools → ToolRegistry → UI / Tool Manager / Automation
A Registry is a component, not a contract. Contracts describe interfaces or expected behavior; a Registry maintains records, resolves identifiers, and applies uniqueness rules. It should live in the layer responsible for managing Tools (e.g., a Tools subsystem), not inside contracts/. The definitive location depends on who owns the lifecycle, discovery, and registration of Tools: the module framework or a specific application subsystem. That responsibility should be defined before fixing the file path.

### 15.1 Identity Conflicts
The Registry must define clear rules for identifier uniqueness, conflicts between modules and plugins, priority, replacement, and deactivation. If two plugins attempt to register rhinoplasty.add_landmark, the Registry must have a deterministic policy. The preferred policy is to reject duplicate identifiers, unless an explicit override mechanism has been defined.

### 15.2 Manifest and Implementation Resolution
When a Tool is declared in a plugin manifest, the framework must resolve the manifest entry to an implementation. An explicit entry point is recommended.

    yaml
    tools:
      - id: rhinoplasty.add_landmark
        label: Add Landmark
        category: annotation
        entry_point: rhinoplasty.tools.add_landmark:AddLandmarkTool

The Registry resolves entry_point via the plugin loader. Declarative metadata alone is not a complete Tool definition; the implementation is authoritative. Information that depends on code or context should not be duplicated in the manifest.

### 15.3 Tool Discovery
Declaring a Tool and deciding where it is presented are distinct responsibilities.


    Module              → declares functionalities and Tools
    Tool Registry/Manager → registers, discovers, resolves, manages Tools
    UI / Editor / Toolbar → chooses which Tools to present and where

A Tool may exist in the Registry without being currently visible. It may be used by a contextual menu, shortcut, workflow, or another interface without requiring its definition to change. The Editor declares which modalities/contexts it accepts, and the Registry filters compatible Tools. This is more flexible than having the Tool declare "I belong to Editor X".

## 16. Tools Provided by Modules and Plugins
Each module may provide the Tools required to expose its functionality.

    Cephalometry Module
    Tools: Add Landmark, Move Landmark, Delete Landmark, Measure Distance, Measure Angle

A module may contain:


    Module
    ├── Commands
    ├── Tools
    ├── Services
    └── Capabilities
However, the module should not directly own the Workspace's Areas or Editors. The relationship with the UI occurs through the appropriate UI, Layout, or Module Specification contracts.

Plugins may register Tools in the same way as internal modules.


    Plugin → ToolRegistry → Custom Tool

This allows third parties to add functionality without modifying the CranioZ Core. Examples:


    AI Segmentation Plugin
    └── Automatic Segmentation Tool
    
    Rhinoplasty Plugin
    ├── Nasal Landmark Tool
    ├── Nasal Osteotomy Tool
    └── Soft Tissue Simulation Tool
Plugin Tools should use the same contracts as native Tools whenever possible. A plugin manifest may declare metadata and entry points (see §15.2).

## 17. Tool and Capability
A Capability describes a capability offered by a module or component. A Tool represents a way to use a capability through an action or interaction.


    Capability: cephalometric_analysis
    
    Tools:
    ├── Add Landmark
    ├── Measure Angle
    └── Calculate Analysis
A Capability answers: "Does the system have this capability?" A Tool answers: "What action or interaction can the user use to access it?"

A Capability may exist without a corresponding Tool. A generic interaction Tool such as Select may not represent a specific clinical Capability.

## 18. Tool and MCP
A CranioZ Tool and an MCP Tool are not the same abstraction. A CranioZ Tool represents an application action or interaction; an MCP Tool represents an operation exposed to an external agent through the MCP protocol. Both may use the same application layer.

    UI Tool                         MCP Tool
      │                                │
      ▼                                ▼
    Command                         Command
      │                                │
      ▼                                ▼
    Application Service             Application Service
      │                                │
      ▼                                ▼
    Domain                          Domain

MCP does not need to control the UI directly. An agent could request create_osteotomy(...), and the application would execute the same fundamental operation used by the graphical interface, passing through the same validations. An adapter-based architecture avoids duplication and allows the UI and automation to share application logic.

## 19. Security and Validation
Tools should not be considered a sufficient security or validation boundary. Important operations should also be validated in the application and domain layers.

    UI Tool → Command → Application Validation → Domain Validation → Operation

This is necessary because Commands may be triggered by mechanisms other than the UI. The system should consider, when applicable: input validation, permissions, authorization, auditing, operational limits, and confirmation of destructive operations. ToolContext may inform permissions, but effective authorization remains in the application and domain layers. This becomes especially important when Commands may be triggered by automation, scripts, or MCP.

## 20. Examples
### 20.1 Landmark Tool

    Tool:      Add Landmark
    
    Context:
    ├── active patient
    ├── active 3D editor
    └── valid anatomical model
    
    Interaction:
    └── user clicks on model
    
    Result:
    └── AddLandmarkCommand

    Add Landmark Tool
            │ user interaction
            ▼
    Landmark Position
            ▼
    AddLandmarkCommand
            ▼
    Application Service
            ▼
    Cephalometric Landmark
            ▼
    Scene / Project
The Tool does not need to know the persistence details of the Landmark.

### 20.2 Osteotomy Tool

    Define Osteotomy Tool
            ▼
    User selects anatomical region
            ▼
    Interactive preview
            ▼
    User confirms
            ▼
    CreateOsteotomyCommand
            ▼
    Application Service
            ▼
    Osteotomy Domain Object
            ▼
    Scene Update
The same operation may later be triggered by a Toolbar, Menu, Shortcut, Workflow, Script, or MCP. The osteotomy logic does not need to be duplicated for each activation mechanism.

## 21. Code Organization
Contract definitions should remain in the framework's module infrastructure.


    src/cranioz/modules/
    ├── base/
    │   ├── module.py
    │   ├── module_state.py
    │   └── lifecycle.py
    │
    ├── contracts/
    │   ├── command.py
    │   ├── tool.py
    │   ├── tool_context.py
    │   ├── tool_result.py
    │   └── tool_session.py
    │
    └── specs/
        └── module_spec.py
The Tool contract should remain small and stable. Specific implementations should remain in the modules that use them.


    src/cranioz/modules/
    └── cephalometry/
        ├── tools/
        │   ├── add_landmark.py
        │   ├── move_landmark.py
        │   └── measure_angle.py
        ├── commands/
        └── services/
The Registry is not a contract and should live in the layer responsible for managing Tools, outside contracts/.

## 22. Architectural Principles
* A Tool is an interaction interface. It is not the domain and should not contain complex clinical or geometric logic.
* A Tool is not a Command. A Tool may create or execute a Command, but the Command remains independent of the UI.
* A Tool does not structurally belong to an Area. It may be presented in any compatible Area or Editor.
* A Tool should be contextual. Its availability may depend on the current project, selection, Editor, or module state.
* A Tool should have a stable identity. Its identifier is independent of its label and interface position.
* A Tool should be discoverable. Tools are registered and queried by the framework.
* A Tool should be reusable. The same operation is triggerable by different mechanisms.
* An interactive Tool should have its own lifecycle. Temporary interaction state is separated from the Tool's permanent definition.
* Validation should not depend on the UI. Important operations are validated in the application and domain layers.
* Plugins may provide Tools. The mechanism works for internal modules and external extensions.
* Tools should not know the Workspace organization. The Tool defines the interaction; the Workspace determines where it is presented.
* Commands should remain independent of Tools. The same operation is usable by the UI, workflows, scripts, and automation.
* Tools should be automation-compatible. The architecture allows the same operations to be used by workflows, scripts, and MCP.
* Threading must be explicit. Tool interaction runs on the UI thread; Commands declare their execution mode; the CommandExecutor decides how to dispatch.

The UI must not inspect concrete modalities. Availability and enablement are resolved by the framework.


======================================================================
FILE: docs\architecture\ui\workspace\workspace-testing.md
======================================================================

# Workspace — Testing Strategy

> **Status:** Draft
> **Related:** [workspace.md](../architecture/workspace.md),
> [workspace-implementation.md](implementation/workspace-implementation.md)
> **Scope:** How the Workspace subsystem is tested

---

## 1. Purpose

This document defines **how** the Workspace is tested: which tools, which
categories of tests, which fixtures, and which quality thresholds.

It is meant to be practical: any contributor should be able to read this
and write a valid test for the Workspace.

---

## 2. Testing Principles

Tests follow the project's engineering principles:

| Principle | How it applies to testing |
|---|---|
| #11 — Determinism | Tests are deterministic; no flaky tests allowed. |
| #12 — Performance is clinical | Layout performance is measured, not assumed. |
| #13 — Documentation is code | Test names document intent. |
| #15 — Errors are information | Tests assert on error messages, not just exceptions. |

Additionally:

- **Test behavior, not implementation.** Tests should survive refactoring.
- **One concept per test.** A test with "and" in its name is two tests.
- **Fail fast.** A failing test should point to the exact broken behavior.
- **No QApplication state leaks.** Each test cleans up after itself.

---

## 3. Tools

| Tool | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **pytest** | Test runner and assertion framework |
| **pytest-qt** | Qt widget testing, `QApplication` lifecycle, signals |
| **pytest-cov** | Coverage measurement |
| **pytest-benchmark** | Performance benchmarks |
| **hypothesis** | Property-based testing for layout invariants |
| **mypy** | Static type checking (runs in CI, not in tests) |

All tools are already installed via `cranioz[test]`.

---

## 4. Test Categories

The Workspace tests are organized in four categories:

| Category | Purpose | Speed | Qt required? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Unit** | Test a single class in isolation | Fast (<10ms) | Usually no |
| **Widget** | Test a widget's behavior | Fast (~50ms) | Yes |
| **Integration** | Test multiple components together | Medium (~200ms) | Yes |
| **Visual** | Compare rendered output | Slow (>1s) | Yes |

Markers:

```python
@pytest.mark.unit
@pytest.mark.qt
@pytest.mark.integration
@pytest.mark.visual
@pytest.mark.slow
```

Run subsets:

```bash
pytest -m "unit"                  # only unit tests
pytest -m "not slow"              # everything except slow tests
pytest -m "qt and not visual"     # only widget tests
```

---

## 5. Folder Structure

Test files mirror the source layout:

```text
tests/
├── conftest.py
├── unit/
│   └── ui/
│       ├── test_placement.py
│       ├── test_area.py
│       ├── test_area_manager.py
│       ├── test_editor_host.py
│       ├── test_editor_registry.py
│       ├── test_layout_tree.py
│       ├── test_region.py
│       └── test_overlay.py
├── integration/
│   └── ui/
│       ├── test_workspace.py
│       ├── test_main_window.py
│       ├── test_layout_builder.py
│       ├── test_layout_renderer.py
│       └── test_layout_io.py
└── data/
    └── fixtures/
        └── layout_samples/
            ├── single_central.json
            ├── left_central_right.json
            ├── four_areas.json
            ├── empty.json
            └── unknown_areas.json
```

**Rule:** a test file's name mirrors the source file it tests.

| Source | Test |
|---|---|
| `ui/workspace/placement.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_placement.py` |
| `ui/workspace/area.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_area.py` |
| `ui/workspace/area_manager.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_area_manager.py` |
| `ui/editors/editor_host.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_editor_host.py` |
| `ui/editors/editor_registry.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_editor_registry.py` |
| `ui/workspace/layout_tree.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_layout_tree.py` |
| `ui/workspace/region.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_region.py` |
| `ui/workspace/overlay.py` | `tests/unit/ui/test_overlay.py` |
| `ui/workspace/workspace.py` | `tests/integration/ui/test_workspace.py` |
| `ui/workspace/layout_builder.py` | `tests/integration/ui/test_layout_builder.py` |
| `ui/workspace/layout_renderer.py` | `tests/integration/ui/test_layout_renderer.py` |
| `ui/main_window.py` | `tests/integration/ui/test_main_window.py` |

---

## 6. Fixtures

### 6.1 Global fixtures (`../../../../tests/conftest.py`)

```python
import pytest
from PySide6.QtWidgets import QApplication, QLabel

from cranioz.ui.editors.base_editor import Editor
from cranioz.ui.workspace.areas.area import Area
from cranioz.ui.workspace.areas.area_manager import AreaManager
from cranioz.ui.workspace.workspace import Workspace


@pytest.fixture(scope="session")
def qapp():
    """Session-wide QApplication (required by Qt)."""
    app = QApplication.instance()
    if app is None:
        app = QApplication([])
    yield app


@pytest.fixture
def workspace(qapp):
    """Fresh Workspace instance."""
    ws = Workspace()
    yield ws
    ws.deleteLater()


@pytest.fixture
def area_manager():
    """Fresh AreaManager instance."""
    return AreaManager()


class DummyEditor(Editor):
    """Minimal concrete Editor for tests."""

    def __init__(self, editor_id: str = "editor.dummy", title: str = "Dummy"):
        super().__init__(editor_id=editor_id, title=title)
        self.set_widget(QLabel(f"[{title}]"))


@pytest.fixture
def make_editor():
    """Factory to create minimal Editors for tests."""

    def _factory(editor_id: str = "editor.dummy") -> Editor:
        return DummyEditor(editor_id=editor_id, title=editor_id)

    return _factory


@pytest.fixture
def make_area(qapp, make_editor):
    """Factory to create minimal Areas for tests."""

    def _factory(area_id: str, title: str | None = None) -> Area:
        area = Area(id=area_id, title=title or area_id)
        area.editor_host.add_editor(make_editor())
        return area

    return _factory
```

### 6.2 Fixture rules

- **`qapp` is session-scoped.** Creating multiple `QApplication` instances
  breaks Qt. Always use the same one.
- **Widget fixtures are function-scoped.** Each test gets a fresh widget.
- **Always clean up.** Use `deleteLater()` on Qt widgets to avoid leaking.
- **No global state.** Fixtures must not depend on module-level variables.

---

## 7. Example Tests

### 7.1 Unit test — `Placement`

```python
# tests/unit/ui/test_placement.py
import pytest

from cranioz.ui.workspace.areas.placement import Placement


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_placement_enum_has_expected_members():
    expected = {"LEFT", "CENTRAL", "RIGHT", "BOTTOM"}
    actual = {member.name for member in Placement}
    assert actual == expected


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_placement_enum_values_are_lowercase_strings():
    for member in Placement:
        assert member.value == member.name.lower()
```

### 7.2 Unit test — `AreaManager`

```python
# tests/unit/ui/test_area_manager.py
import pytest


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_register_adds_area(area_manager, make_area):
    area = make_area("test")
    area_manager.register(area)
    assert area_manager.get("test") is area


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_duplicate_id_raises(area_manager, make_area):
    area_manager.register(make_area("dup"))
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="already registered"):
        area_manager.register(make_area("dup"))


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_unregister_removes_area(area_manager, make_area):
    area_manager.register(make_area("test"))
    area_manager.unregister("test")
    assert area_manager.get("test") is None


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_unregister_unknown_id_is_noop(area_manager):
    area_manager.unregister("does-not-exist")
    assert area_manager.get("does-not-exist") is None
```

### 7.3 Widget test — `Area`

```python
# tests/unit/ui/test_area.py
import pytest

from cranioz.ui.workspace.areas.area import Area


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_area_has_id_and_title(qtbot):
    area = Area(id="area.test", title="Test")
    qtbot.addWidget(area)
    assert area.id == "area.test"
    assert area.title == "Test"


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_area_has_editor_host(qtbot):
    area = Area(id="area.test", title="Test")
    qtbot.addWidget(area)
    assert area.editor_host is not None


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_area_does_not_store_placement(qtbot):
    """Area must not expose `placement` — it is construction metadata."""
    area = Area(id="area.test", title="Test")
    qtbot.addWidget(area)
    assert not hasattr(area, "placement")
```

### 7.4 Unit test — `EditorHost`

```python
# tests/unit/ui/test_editor_host.py
import pytest

from cranioz.ui.editors.editor_host import EditorHost


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_add_editor(qtbot, make_editor):
    host = EditorHost()
    qtbot.addWidget(host)
    editor = make_editor("editor.a")
    host.add_editor(editor)
    assert editor in host.list_editors()


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_remove_editor(qtbot, make_editor):
    host = EditorHost()
    qtbot.addWidget(host)
    editor = make_editor("editor.a")
    host.add_editor(editor)
    host.remove_editor(editor.id)
    assert host.get_editor(editor.id) is None


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_set_active_editor(qtbot, make_editor):
    host = EditorHost()
    qtbot.addWidget(host)
    a = make_editor("editor.a")
    b = make_editor("editor.b")
    host.add_editor(a)
    host.add_editor(b)
    host.set_active(a.id)
    assert host.active_editor is a


@pytest.mark.qt
def test_detach_does_not_destroy_editor(qtbot, make_editor):
    host = EditorHost()
    qtbot.addWidget(host)
    editor = make_editor("editor.a")
    host.add_editor(editor)
    detached = host.detach_editor(editor.id)
    assert detached is editor
```

### 7.5 Unit test — `EditorRegistry`

```python
# tests/unit/ui/test_editor_registry.py
import pytest

from cranioz.ui.editors.editor_registry import EditorRegistry


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_register_and_resolve():
    registry = EditorRegistry()
    registry.register("editor.dummy", DummyEditor)
    assert registry.resolve("editor.dummy") is DummyEditor


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_resolve_unknown_raises():
    registry = EditorRegistry()
    with pytest.raises(KeyError, match="editor.unknown"):
        registry.resolve("editor.unknown")


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_create_returns_instance(qtbot):
    registry = EditorRegistry()
    registry.register("editor.dummy", DummyEditor)
    editor = registry.create("editor.dummy")
    qtbot.addWidget(editor)
    assert isinstance(editor, DummyEditor)
```

### 7.6 Unit test — `LayoutTree.from_specs`

```python
# tests/unit/ui/test_layout_tree.py
import pytest

from cranioz.ui.workspace.layout_tree import Leaf, Split, SplitDirection
from cranioz.ui.workspace.layout_tree import LayoutTree
from cranioz.ui.workspace.areas.placement import Placement


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_from_specs_single_central(make_area):
    specs = [_spec("area.central", Placement.CENTRAL)]
    areas = {"area.central": make_area("area.central")}
    tree = LayoutTree.from_specs(specs, areas)

    assert isinstance(tree.root, Leaf)
    assert tree.root.area.id == "area.central"


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_from_specs_left_central_right(make_area):
    specs = [
        _spec("area.left", Placement.LEFT),
        _spec("area.central", Placement.CENTRAL),
        _spec("area.right", Placement.RIGHT),
    ]
    areas = {s.area_id: make_area(s.area_id) for s in specs}
    tree = LayoutTree.from_specs(specs, areas)

    assert isinstance(tree.root, Split)
    assert tree.root.direction == SplitDirection.HORIZONTAL
    assert len(tree.root.children) == 3


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_from_specs_with_bottom(make_area):
    specs = [
        _spec("area.central", Placement.CENTRAL),
        _spec("area.bottom", Placement.BOTTOM),
    ]
    areas = {s.area_id: make_area(s.area_id) for s in specs}
    tree = LayoutTree.from_specs(specs, areas)

    assert isinstance(tree.root, Split)
    assert tree.root.direction == SplitDirection.VERTICAL


@pytest.mark.unit
def test_from_specs_requires_central(make_area):
    specs = [_spec("area.left", Placement.LEFT)]
    areas = {"area.left": make_area("area.left")}
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="CENTRAL"):
        LayoutTree.from_specs(specs, areas)


def _spec(area_id, placement):
    """Helper to build a minimal AreaSpec for tests."""
    from cranioz.ui.workspace.specs.area_spec import AreaSpec

    return AreaSpec(
        area_id=area_id,
        title=area_id,
        placement=placement,
        editor_ids=["editor.dummy"],
    )
```

### 7.7 Integration test — `Workspace` with `ModuleUISpec`

```python
# tests/integration/ui/test_workspace.py
import pytest

from cranioz.ui.workspace.specs.area_spec import AreaSpec
from cranioz.ui.workspace.module_ui_spec import ModuleUISpec
from cranioz.ui.workspace.areas.placement import Placement
from cranioz.ui.workspace.workspace import Workspace


@pytest.mark.qt
@pytest.mark.integration
def test_workspace_loads_spec_single_central(qtbot):
    ws = Workspace()
    qtbot.addWidget(ws)
    spec = ModuleUISpec(
        areas=[AreaSpec("area.central", "Central", Placement.CENTRAL, ["editor.dummy"])],
    )
    ws.load_spec(spec)
    assert "area.central" in [a.id for a in ws.list_areas()]


@pytest.mark.integration
def test_workspace_loads_spec_left_central_right(qtbot):
    ws = Workspace()
    qtbot.addWidget(ws)
    spec = ModuleUISpec(
        areas=[
            AreaSpec("area.left", "Left", Placement.LEFT, ["editor.dummy"]),
            AreaSpec("area.central", "Central", Placement.CENTRAL, ["editor.dummy"]),
            AreaSpec("area.right", "Right", Placement.RIGHT, ["editor.dummy"]),
        ],
    )
    ws.load_spec(spec)
    assert {a.id for a in ws.list_areas()} == {"area.left", "area.central", "area.right"}
```

### 7.8 Benchmark — layout performance

```python
# tests/integration/ui/test_layout_benchmark.py
import pytest


@pytest.mark.qt
@pytest.mark.benchmark
def test_load_spec_with_20_areas(benchmark, qtbot, make_area):
    from cranioz.ui.workspace.workspace import Workspace

    ws = Workspace()
    qtbot.addWidget(ws)

    areas = [make_area(f"area.{i}") for i in range(20)]

    def add_all():
        for area in areas:
            ws._area_manager.register(area)

    benchmark(add_all)
    # Acceptance: adding 20 areas takes < 100ms on a modern laptop
    assert benchmark.stats["mean"] < 0.1
```

---

## 8. Testing Anti-Patterns

Things **not** to do:

| Anti-pattern | Why it is bad |
|---|---|
| `time.sleep()` in tests | Flaky; use `qtbot.waitSignal` or `qtbot.wait`. |
| Multiple `QApplication` instances | Breaks Qt; use the session-scoped `qapp`. |
| Asserting on `repr()` of widgets | Fragile; assert on properties or state. |
| Testing private methods (`_foo`) | Couples tests to implementation. |
| Catching all exceptions | Hides real failures. |
| Sharing state between tests | Order-dependent test suites are useless. |
| Long-running tests without `@pytest.mark.slow` | Slows the whole suite. |
| Skipping tests without a reason | `@pytest.mark.skip(reason="...")` always. |

---

## 9. Testing the Workspace — Specific Cases

### 9.1 Area registration

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| Register a new Area | Added to `AreaManager`. |
| Register duplicate ID | `ValueError` with clear message. |
| Unregister non-existent ID | No error, no-op. |
| List areas when none registered | Empty list. |

### 9.2 Spec loading

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| `ModuleUISpec` with `[central]` | `Leaf(central)` built. |
| `ModuleUISpec` with `[left, central, right]` | `Split(HORIZONTAL, 3 children)`. |
| `ModuleUISpec` with `[left, central, right, bottom]` | `Split(VERTICAL)` with horizontal sub-split. |
| `ModuleUISpec` without `CENTRAL` | `ValueError` with clear message. |

### 9.3 Visibility

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| Hide a visible Area | Area not visible, still registered. |
| Show a hidden Area | Area visible again. |
| Hide all Areas | Workspace empty but stable. |
| Hide then remove an Area | No error. |

### 9.4 Persistence

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| Save and load empty layout | Round-trip stable. |
| Save and load 4-areas layout | Round-trip stable. |
| Load layout with unknown Area ID | Warning, area skipped. |
| Load layout with missing Area | Default placement applied. |
| Load malformed JSON | `ValueError` with clear message. |

### 9.5 Lifecycle

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| Create Workspace, load spec, destroy | No leaks (use `gc` + `weakref`). |
| Close MainWindow with open Areas | All Areas cleaned up. |
| Reopen project | Areas re-created from layout. |

### 9.6 Module switch

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| Switch to a Module with same `area_id` and same `editor_ids` | Area is **reused**. |
| Switch to a Module with same `area_id` but different `editor_ids` | Area is **reconfigured**. |
| Switch to a Module without a given `area_id` | Area is **destroyed**. |
| Switch to a Module with a new `area_id` | Area is **created**. |

### 9.7 Overlays

| Scenario | Expected |
|---|---|
| Register an Overlay | Overlay is added to `OverlayManager`. |
| Change layout (visibility toggle) | Overlay is **repositioned**. |
| Remove an Overlay | Overlay is unregistered. |

---

## 10. CI Integration

The CI runs tests in a matrix:

```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml (excerpt)
strategy:
  matrix:
    python-version: ["3.12", "3.13"]
    os: [ubuntu-latest, windows-latest, macos-latest]

steps:
  - uses: actions/checkout@v4
  - uses: astral-sh/setup-uv@v3
  - run: uv sync --all-extras
  - run: uv run pytest -m "not slow" --cov=src/cranioz --cov-report=xml
  - run: uv run pytest -m "slow" --benchmark-only
```

**Headless Qt:** set `QT_QPA_PLATFORM=offscreen` in CI.

```yaml
env:
  QT_QPA_PLATFORM: offscreen
```

---

## 11. Coverage Targets

| Scope | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `ui/workspace/` | **≥ 80%** | Per the project's validation criteria. |
| `ui/editors/` | ≥ 70% | EditorHost, EditorRegistry, base Editor. |
| Overall `ui/` | ≥ 60% | Increasing as features are added. |

Coverage is enforced via `--cov-fail-under` in CI for the workspace scope.

---

## 12. Performance Benchmarks

The Workspace has performance criteria derived from the PDF:

| Metric | Target | Test |
|---|---|---|
| Load `ModuleUISpec` with 20 Areas | < 100 ms | `test_load_spec_with_20_areas` |
| Build `LayoutTree` from specs | < 20 ms | `test_build_tree_benchmark` |
| Remove an Area | < 20 ms | `test_remove_area` |
| Save layout | < 10 ms | `test_save_layout_benchmark` |
| Load layout | < 20 ms | `test_load_layout_benchmark` |
| Reposition 3 Overlays | < 10 ms | `test_reposition_overlays_benchmark` |

Benchmarks run on every PR. A regression of more than 20% blocks the PR.

---

## 13. Manual Testing Checklist

Before each release, manually verify:

- [ ] Window opens and closes cleanly on Windows, Linux, macOS.
- [ ] Areas are resizable with the mouse.
- [ ] Areas can be hidden and shown.
- [ ] Layout persists across restarts.
- [ ] Module switch preserves compatible Areas.
- [ ] Editor tabs appear when ≥ 2 Editors.
- [ ] Overlay repositions on window resize.
- [ ] High-DPI display looks correct (150%, 200% scaling).
- [ ] Multiple monitors: floating Areas appear on the correct monitor.
- [ ] Keyboard shortcuts work as expected.
- [ ] No console errors or warnings during normal use.

---

## 14. Definition of Done for Tests

A feature is considered tested when:

- [ ] Unit tests cover the core logic.
- [ ] Widget tests cover the UI behavior.
- [ ] Integration tests cover the interaction with other components.
- [ ] Coverage target is met.
- [ ] No flaky tests.
- [ ] No `time.sleep()` in tests.
- [ ] All tests have clear names describing intent.
- [ ] All tests pass locally and in CI.

---

## 15. Open Questions

### 15.1 Should visual regression tests be added?

**Option A:** Yes — screenshot comparison for the Workspace.
**Option B:** No — defer until the UI is more stable.

**Current lean:** Option B.

### 15.2 Should Hypothesis be used more aggressively?

**Option A:** Yes — property tests for layout invariants.
**Option B:** No — hypothesis is slow and can be flaky with Qt.

**Current lean:** Use Hypothesis only for pure logic (no Qt).

### 15.3 How to test multi-monitor behavior in CI?

CI environments have one virtual screen.

**Current lean:** Mock `QScreen` geometry in tests. Do not attempt to
test actual multi-monitor behavior in CI.



======================================================================
FILE: docs\architecture\ui\workspace\workspace.md
======================================================================

# Workspace
## 1. Concept
The Workspace is the runtime environment where clinical work happens. It is not a single widget nor a single class. It is a composition of Areas — containers that host Editors — and Overlays — floating layers over regions.

### 1.1 What the Workspace is
* A container of Areas.
* A layout coordinator that arranges Areas in a split tree.
* A lifecycle manager for Areas, and a coordinator of the Editor lifecycle
* within those Areas.
* An Overlay manager.
* A configuration target whose layout is persisted per project and per Module.
* A materializer of UI compositions declared by Modules.

### 1.2 The single rule
The Workspace manages Areas and Overlays and coordinates their layout and lifecycle. It may depend on the platform contracts required to materialize and manage them, but it does not own clinical state, Tools, Toolbars, or Module behavior.

If something is not an Area and not an Overlay, the Workspace does not interpret it. Toolbars, Tools and Modules are invisible to the Workspace, but the Workspace may use platform services (LayoutRenderer, EditorRegistry, OverlayManager) to materialize and manage its elements.

### 1.3 Module declares; Workspace materializes

The Module declares the UI composition. The Workspace materializes that
declaration.

The Module does not own Areas or Editors. It describes what it wants
through a `ModuleUISpec`.

The Workspace materializes it:

- Resolves `editor_id` values in the `EditorRegistry`.
- Requests the `EditorRegistry` to create Editor instances.
- Creates `Area` instances from the `AreaSpec`.
- Builds the Area Layout Tree.
- Delegates rendering to the `LayoutRenderer`, which materializes the
  tree as nested `QSplitter` widgets and hosts the Area widgets.
- Positions Overlays over Regions.

Each Area builds its own Editor Layout Tree from the resolved
`EditorLayoutSpec`. The Workspace does not build the Editor Layout Tree.

The Workspace does not know the Module. It only receives the specification.

## 2. Core Concept: Area
An Area is the fundamental building block of the Workspace.

This is Principle #7 (Panels Are Areas): there are no SidePanel, BottomPanel, CentralArea or ToolbarArea classes. There is only Area.

### 2.1 What an Area is

An Area is a container of Editors. It:

- Has a stable identifier within a layout context (e.g., `area.left`,
  `area.central`).
- Has a title (user-facing, translatable via i18n).
- Hosts one or more Editors, organized in its own internal
  Editor Layout Tree.
- Manages its Editors directly: it hosts them, organizes them in its
  internal Editor Layout Tree, and tracks which one is active.
- Has a visibility state (shown/hidden).
- Is positioned by the Area Layout Tree, never by itself.

Every Area follows the same model. There is no special type of Area.
Any Area (`LEFT`, `CENTRAL`, `RIGHT`, `BOTTOM`) may host one or
multiple Editors.

### 2.2 What an Area is NOT

- Not a Toolbar. A Toolbar is a widget inside an Editor (see ADR-0002).
- Not a Module. A Module declares which Areas it wants the Workspace
  to materialize.
- Not a state holder for clinical data. State lives in the Scene.
- Not a manager of other Areas.
- Not an `EditorHost`. There is no `EditorHost` abstraction. The Area
  manages its Editors directly, using its internal Editor Layout Tree
  (see ADR-0001).

### 2.3 Area lifecycle

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `created` | Instance exists, not yet in the Workspace. |
| `registered` | Known to the `AreaManager`. |
| `placed` | Assigned to a position in the Area Layout Tree. |
| `activated` | Visible and receiving user input. |
| `deactivated` | Hidden but still registered. |
| `unregistered` | Removed from the `AreaManager`. |
| `destroyed` | Instance disposed. |

**Relationship with the Editor lifecycle.** The Area is the trigger for
the Editor lifecycle. When an Area is created from an `AreaSpec`, the
Editors declared in its `EditorLayoutSpec` are instantiated, registered,
and inserted. When an Area is destroyed, its Editors are removed and
disposed. Editor activation is independent of Area activation.

## 3. Workspace Architecture
### 3.1 High-level composition
The Workspace is a split tree of Areas, not a fixed set of panels.

```
text
+-------------------------------------------------------+
|                     MainWindow                        |
+-------------------------------------------------------+
|  +----------+----------------------+----------+       |
|  | Area     | Area                 | Area     |       |
|  | left     | central              | right    |       |
|  +----------+----------------------+----------+       |
|                                                       |
|  +---------------------------------------------------+|
|  | Area — bottom                                     ||
|  +---------------------------------------------------+|
+-------------------------------------------------------+`
```

Every visible rectangle is an Area. Each Area hosts its own Editor Layout Tree, containing the Editors assigned to it.

The Workspace does not define a default layout. It is an empty skeleton until a Module declares the UI composition it wants.

Who decides how many and which Areas exist is the Module loaded by the Flow:

Module	UI composition
Patient	[central]
3D Mesh Editing	[left, central, right]
Tomography	[left, central, right, bottom]
The tree is the single source of truth for structure.

### 3.2 Relationship diagram
Key points:

- The Workspace knows `Area`, `AreaManager`, `LayoutTree`, `LayoutState`,
  `OverlayManager`, `EditorRegistry` and `EditorCatalog`.
- The Workspace has no reference to `Scene`, `Editor`, `Toolbar` or `Module`
  (see ADR-0001 and ADR-0002).
- The Workspace materializes a `ModuleUISpec`, but does not know the Module.
- `AreaSpec` references Editors via `EditorLayoutSpec`, which contains
  `editor_id` (string) at its leaves.
- The `EditorRegistry` resolves `editor_id` to concrete classes and creates
  instances.
- The `EditorCatalog` filters which Editors are available in the current
  context.
- The `LayoutTree` is an N-ary tree of `Leaf` and `Split`.
- Each Area builds and hosts its own internal Editor Layout Tree (same N-ary
  model). The Workspace does not build the Editor Layout Tree.
- Structure lives in the trees; presentation state lives in `LayoutState`.
- A Region is derived geometrically from the Area Layout Tree.
- An Overlay covers a Region.

The `AreaManager` is the registry of Areas within the Workspace. It handles
registration, unregistration, and lookup by `area_id`.

### 3.3 Application chrome
Some visual elements are neither Areas nor Overlays:

- The native OS menu bar (if any).
- Native window controls — only if they are not embedded in the Top Bar.
  In CranioZ, window controls are typically embedded in the right segment
  of the Top Bar.

The native OS menu bar belongs to the `MainWindow`. Native window controls belong to the `MainWindow` only if they are not embedded in the Top Bar. When embedded in the Top Bar, they belong to the Workspace.

There is no status bar. The traditional status bar role is served by the Console Editor, hosted in the Bottom Area, when the Module declares it. For details on the Top Bar (Painel Superior) — a structural component of the Workspace, not an Overlay — and its 3 segments, see `top-bar.md`.

For details on the `steps_panel` — a shared Overlay hosted by the Workspace — see `steps_panel.md`.

### 3.4 Data flow

The Workspace does not mutate clinical state. It only presents it.

```
User action → Editor → Command → CommandBus → CommandHandler → Transaction
↓
Scene (mutation)
↓
commit
↓
EventBus
↓
Editors → redraw
```

Read/write separation:

- **Read:** the Editor accesses the Scene via `SceneProvider` (a read-only
  API over the Scene). It never receives a mutable reference.
- **Write:** the Editor publishes `Command`s to the `CommandBus`. It never
  mutates the Scene directly.

The `CommandHandler` (or `CommandExecutor`) is responsible for validating
the Command, opening a transaction, applying the changes to the Domain/Scene,
committing, and publishing the events. Editors and Tools never interact with
the transaction directly.

This keeps the Scene as the single source of truth for clinical data.

**UI operations** (switching active Areas, switching active Editors within
an Area, changing visibility, resizing) are not clinical Commands. They
update `LayoutState` and are persisted with the project. They are not mixed
with the clinical undo/redo history.

- The **Area** manages which Editor is active within it.
- The **Workspace** manages which Area is active globally.

### 3.5 The Module declares the UI composition
The Workspace does not decide which Areas exist. Each Module declares its UI composition through a ModuleUISpec.
```
python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ModuleUISpec:
    areas: list[AreaSpec]
    overlays: list[OverlaySpec]

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AreaSpec:
    area_id: str
    title: str
    placement: Placement
    editor_layout: EditorLayoutSpec

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class EditorLayoutSpec:
    type: LayoutType                          # LEAF | SPLIT
    direction: SplitDirection | None = None   # HORIZONTAL | VERTICAL
    children: tuple["EditorLayoutSpec", ...] = ()
    editor_id: str | None = None              # when type == LEAF
```
Important points:

Placement is semantic metadata. It is used to associate Areas with Regions and to build the initial tree.

It is preserved after construction, because Regions depend on it.

AreaSpec declares its Editors through an EditorLayoutSpec, not a flat list.

The Module does not import the Editor classes.

The EditorRegistry resolves the IDs to concrete classes.

Validation rules (enforced before materialization):

LEAF must have editor_id and must not have children.

SPLIT must have direction and at least two children, and must not have editor_id.

Every editor_id must resolve in the EditorRegistry.

Placement must be one of LEFT, CENTRAL, RIGHT, BOTTOM.

A ModuleUISpec must declare at least CENTRAL.

If any rule is violated, the Workspace rejects the load with a clear error. It does not fall back silently.

Example:
```
text
Orthognathic Module declares:

ModuleUISpec(
    areas=[
        AreaSpec("area.left", "Lists", Placement.LEFT,
            EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.object_list")),

        AreaSpec("area.central", "Editing", Placement.CENTRAL,
            EditorLayoutSpec.split(HORIZONTAL, (
                EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.viewport_3d"),
                EditorLayoutSpec.split(VERTICAL, (
                    EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.mpr_axial"),
                    EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.mpr_sagittal"),
                )),
            ))),

        AreaSpec("area.right", "Tools", Placement.RIGHT,
            EditorLayoutSpec.split(VERTICAL, (
                EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.properties"),
                EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.material"),
            ))),

        AreaSpec("area.bottom", "Consoles", Placement.BOTTOM,
            EditorLayoutSpec.split(HORIZONTAL, (
                EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.console"),
                EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.history"),
                EditorLayoutSpec.leaf("editor.ai_assistant"),
            ))),
    ],
)
```
Placement rules:

    CENTRAL — the center of the Area Layout Tree. There may be multiple Central Areas in a complex composition.
  
    LEFT — flanks the central region on the left.
  
    RIGHT — flanks the central region on the right.
  
    BOTTOM — sits below the horizontal row.

Switching Modules: the Workspace receives a new ModuleUISpec and reconciles its current layout against it (see §6.4).

### 3.6 EditorRegistry

The `EditorRegistry` resolves `editor_id` to concrete Editor implementations.

```
python
class EditorRegistry:
    def register(self, editor_id: str, editor_type: type[Editor]) -> None: ...
    def resolve(self, editor_id: str) -> type[Editor]: ...
    def create(self, editor_id: str) -> Editor: ...
```
The Registry registers types, not instances. It answers:

"Which Editor types exist and how are they created?"

Error contracts:

register: raises ValueError if editor_id is already registered.

resolve: raises KeyError if editor_id is unknown.

create: raises KeyError if editor_id is unknown.

Availability is a separate concern, handled by the EditorCatalog. The
EditorCatalog filters registered Editors by:

The active Scene.

The active project.

The current Area.

Required capabilities and data.

Registry population:

System Editors register at boot.

Modules register their Editors when loaded.

Plugins register their Editors when activated.

When a Module or Plugin is unloaded or deactivated, its Editors are
unregistered from the EditorRegistry. Instances already created are
disposed following the Editor lifecycle.

## 4. Editor

An Editor is the functional unit hosted by an Area. There is no distinction
between "Viewport", "Panel" or "Editor". They are all Editors. A "Panel" is
a semantic specialization of an Editor (e.g., `editor_kind = PANEL`), not a
separate concept.

The relationship between Area and Editor is defined in ADR-0001. The
ownership of Toolbars is defined in ADR-0002.

For full details on the Editor lifecycle and catalog, see `editors.md`.

### 4.1 What an Editor is

An Editor is a widget that:

- Renders or edits a specific type of content.
- Receives Scene events via `SceneQuery` (read-only API).
- Emits user intent via `Command` (write).
- Declares and materializes its own Toolbar(s).

An Editor is responsible for:

- Defining a functional editing or visualization experience.
- Presenting a specific type of content.
- Declaring and materializing its own Toolbar(s).
- Translating user interactions into Commands.
- Reacting to relevant changes in application state.

An Editor is **not** responsible for:

- Determining its position in the interface.
- Creating or managing Areas.
- Defining the Workspace layout.
- Implementing clinical logic belonging to Modules.
- Directly modifying the clinical state of the Scene.

The Workspace does not know what an Editor does. In fact, it has no
reference to the Editor instance at all.

### 4.2 Editor lifecycle

The Editor lifecycle of a specific **instance**:

| Stage | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `instantiated` | The instance is created by the `EditorRegistry`. |
| `inserted` | The instance is added to an Area's Editor Layout Tree. |
| `activated` | The instance is the active Editor within the Area. |
| `deactivated` | The instance is no longer active but is still hosted. |
| `removed` | The instance is removed from the composition. |
| `disposed` | The instance is destroyed and its resources are released. |

Separately, the **type** is registered in the `EditorRegistry` when the
system, a Module, or a Plugin calls `register(editor_id, editor_type)`.

**Instance vs type:** the `EditorRegistry` maintains **types**; each
**Area** maintains the **instances** it hosts. The Workspace has no
reference to Editor instances.

Initialization with the Scene occurs at insertion (once). Later activations
sync the presentation state, without re-initializing heavy resources.

**Relationship with the Area lifecycle:** the Editor lifecycle is triggered
by the Area lifecycle. When an Area is created from an `AreaSpec`, the
Editors declared in its `EditorLayoutSpec` are instantiated and inserted.
When an Area is destroyed, its Editors are removed and disposed. Activation
is independent: an Editor can be activated or deactivated without the Area
being affected.

### 4.3 Editor catalog

The system maintains two related structures:

- **`EditorRegistry`** — the registry of all Editor **types** known to the
  system. It answers: "Which Editor types exist and how are they created?"
- **`EditorCatalog`** — the context-aware filter over the `EditorRegistry`.
  It answers: "Which registered Editors can be used in the current context?"

The `EditorRegistry` is populated by:

- System Editors, registered at boot.
- Editors registered by Modules, when loaded.
- Editors registered by Plugins, when activated.

The `EditorCatalog` presents the subset of registered Editors available in
the current context, respecting:

- The active Scene.
- The active project.
- The current Area.
- Required capabilities and data.

Editors are organized by category in the catalog:

| Category | Editors |
|---|---|
| View | `Viewport3DEditor`, `MPRViewerEditor` |
| Mesh | `MeshEditor`, `NodeEditor` |
| Data | `PropertiesEditor`, `SceneEditor`, `DocumentEditor` |
| Console | `ConsoleEditor` |
| Aux | `AIConsoleEditor`, `HistoryEditor`, `AnimationEditor` |

### 4.4 Where an Editor sits

The Editor does not decide where it sits. The Area hosts it. The Area's
internal Editor Layout Tree determines its position.

Who decides which Editor goes in which Area:

- The Module, when declaring its `AreaSpec` (initial composition).
- The user, when adding or removing an Editor in an existing Area.

The Area materializes the composition: it builds its internal Editor Layout
Tree from the resolved `EditorLayoutSpec` (initial) or from user actions
(subsequent changes).

Typical conventions (not rules):

| Editor | Where it typically sits |
|---|---|
| `PropertiesEditor` | Right or Bottom |
| `ConsoleEditor` | Bottom |
| `NodeEditor` | Central |
| `Viewport3DEditor` | Central |
| `MPRViewerEditor` | Central |

**Instance policy:** each Editor is a new instance. Two `Viewport3DEditor`
are two independent instances. If the same `editor_id` appears multiple
times in the same composition, multiple independent instances are created.
This is intentional: the user may want two independent Viewport3DEditors
showing different views.

## 5. Layout Model — Dual N-ary Split Trees
### 5.1 The model
The Workspace has two kinds of layout trees, both following the same N-ary split tree model:

Area Layout Tree (external) — organizes the Areas of the Workspace.

Editor Layout Trees (internal) — each Area has its own, organizing the Editors hosted by that Area.

Node types (both trees):

A Leaf contains exactly one element (Area or Editor).

A Split contains two or more children and a direction (HORIZONTAL or VERTICAL).

```
text
Workspace
├── Area Layout Tree (external)
│   ├── Area LEFT      → Editor Layout Tree (internal)
│   ├── Area CENTRAL   → Editor Layout Tree (internal)
│   ├── Area RIGHT     → Editor Layout Tree (internal)
│   └── Area BOTTOM    → Editor Layout Tree (internal)
└── Overlays
```
This is the only layout mechanism. No slots, no predefined templates, no grid.

There is no special Area. LEFT, CENTRAL, RIGHT, and BOTTOM follow the same model.

### 5.2 Initial state
The Workspace starts empty. There is no pre-built tree.

Both trees are built when the first Module is loaded, from the ModuleUISpec.

Examples:

Patient Module ([central] with one Editor):
```
text
Area Layout Tree: Leaf(area.central)
Editor Layout Tree (area.central): Leaf(editor.patient)
```
Tomography Module (central with 2×2 Editors, right with 2 Editors, bottom with 3 Editors):
```
text
Area Layout Tree:
Split(VERTICAL)
├── Split(HORIZONTAL)
│   ├── Leaf(area.left)
│   ├── Leaf(area.central)
│   └── Leaf(area.right)
└── Leaf(area.bottom)
```

Editor Layout Tree (area.central):
```
Split(HORIZONTAL)
├── Leaf(editor.mpr_axial)
└── Split(VERTICAL)
    ├── Leaf(editor.mpr_sagittal)
    └── Leaf(editor.viewport_3d)
```

Editor Layout Tree (area.right):
```
Split(VERTICAL)
├── Leaf(editor.properties)
└── Leaf(editor.material)
```

Editor Layout Tree (area.bottom):
```
Split(HORIZONTAL)
├── Leaf(editor.console)
├── Leaf(editor.history)
└── Leaf(editor.ai_assistant)
```

### 5.3 Structure vs presentation state
The trees are the single source of truth for structure. They are not the single source of truth for all visual state.

The following are stored separately in LayoutState:

State	Purpose
Proportions	QSplitter sizes per node
Visibility	Which Areas are shown or hidden
Active Editor per Area	Which Editor receives input in each Area
The following are stored separately in OverlayState:

State	Purpose
Presence	Which Overlays exist
Configuration	Overlay-specific settings
Anchoring	Which Region each Overlay targets
These state models may be serialized together with the trees, but their responsibilities are distinct.

### 5.4 Resizing and reconciliation
Each Split (in either tree) is rendered as a QSplitter. The user can drag the dividers to resize adjacent elements.

Reconciliation rule:

The tree determines the structure.

LayoutState stores proportions and preferences.

The LayoutRenderer materializes both.

User interactions update the model that owns the value.

The renderer never silently alters structure to reflect a widget.

Without this rule, when a user drags a QSplitter divider, the new proportion would be lost on the next render.

### 5.5 Rendering
Rendering both trees into Qt widgets is the responsibility of the LayoutRenderer.

The layout trees are pure data models, with no Qt dependency. The LayoutRenderer is the Anti-Corruption Layer between the models and Qt.

The LayoutRenderer renders recursively:

An Area Layout Tree Split → QSplitter at the Workspace level.

An Area Layout Tree Leaf → the Area widget.

Inside each Area, its Editor Layout Tree Split → QSplitter.

Its Editor Layout Tree Leaf → the Editor widget.

### 5.6 Persistence
Layout persistence is keyed by context:
```

text
project_id + module_id + layout_schema_version
```
Reason: different Modules require different compositions. A tomography flow and an orthognathic planning flow need distinct layouts. The Workspace must remember the personalization for each Module independently.

Serialization format:
```
json
{
  "layout_schema_version": 1,
  "module_id": "orthognathic",
  "area_tree": {
    "type": "split",
    "direction": "vertical",
    "children": [
      {
        "type": "split",
        "direction": "horizontal",
        "children": [
          { "type": "leaf", "area_id": "area.left" },
          { "type": "leaf", "area_id": "area.central" },
          { "type": "leaf", "area_id": "area.right" }
        ]
      },
      { "type": "leaf", "area_id": "area.bottom" }
    ]
  },
  "editor_trees": {
    "area.left": { "type": "leaf", "editor_id": "editor.object_list" },
    "area.central": {
      "type": "split", "direction": "horizontal",
      "children": [
        { "type": "leaf", "editor_id": "editor.viewport_3d" },
        { "type": "split", "direction": "vertical",
          "children": [
            { "type": "leaf", "editor_id": "editor.mpr_axial" },
            { "type": "leaf", "editor_id": "editor.mpr_sagittal" }
          ]
        }
      ]
    }
  },
  "layout_state": {
    "proportions": { "central_split": [0.7, 0.3] },
    "visibility": { "area.left": true, "area.right": true },
    "active_editor_per_area": {
      "area.central": "editor.viewport_3d"
    }
  },
  "overlay_state": {
    "top_bar": { "visible": true }
  }
}
```
This JSON is stored inside the project file, not in user settings.

Precedence when opening a project:

Persisted layout for (project_id, module_id) if compatible with the current ModuleUISpec.

Default layout declared by the ModuleUISpec.

System fallback (single central Area with a single Editor).

If the persisted layout is incompatible with the current ModuleUISpec, the Workspace reconciles it (see §6.4). If reconciliation is not possible, it rebuilds from the ModuleUISpec and reports the change.

If the ModuleUISpec itself is invalid, the Workspace rejects the load with a clear error. The system fallback is reserved for controlled recovery, not for masking configuration errors.

5.7 Why an N-ary tree
Simple: two node types.

Recursive: each subtree is a complete layout.

Composable: any element can be split; any split can be closed.

Persistable: JSON maps directly to the tree.

Direct mapping to Qt: each Split is rendered as a QSplitter; each Leaf as a widget.

### 5.8 Evolution
The tree model is stable. Future operations:

split and close of nodes.

Drag-and-drop to reorder Leaves.

Floating Areas.

Multiple Workspaces.

None of these operations changes the tree model.

## 6. Workspace Lifecycle
### 6.1 Startup
```
text
QApplication starts
    ↓
MainWindow created
    ↓
Workspace created (empty — no trees)
    ↓
Modules loaded via ModuleRegistry
    ↓
Initial Flow is selected
    ↓
First Module is loaded
    ↓
Workspace reads the Module's ModuleUISpec and validates it
    ↓
LayoutBuilder builds the Area Layout Tree + Editor Layout Trees
    ↓
LayoutRenderer renders as nested QSplitters
    ↓
Workspace positions Overlays over Regions
    ↓
Workspace shown to the user
```

### 6.2 Opening a project
```
text
Project opened
    ↓
Scene loaded from JSON
    ↓
Project Flow is selected
    ↓
Flow Module is loaded
    ↓
Persisted layout for (project_id, module_id) is loaded (if it exists)
    ↓
If compatible with the ModuleUISpec:
    Workspace reconciles the current trees against it
Else:
    LayoutBuilder builds the trees from the ModuleUISpec
    ↓
LayoutRenderer renders the trees
    ↓
Workspace positions Overlays over Regions
    ↓
Editors redraw from Scene state
```

### 6.3 Shutdown
```
text
User closes the window
    ↓
Workspace serializes:
    - Area Layout Tree
    - Editor Layout Trees
    - LayoutState
    - OverlayState
    ↓
Scene saved (if dirty)
    ↓
QApplication exits
```

### 6.4 Module switch — layout resolution
Rule: the layout is remembered per Module, not globally.
```
text
User selects another Module
    ↓
Workspace loads the ModuleUISpec and validates it
    ↓
If a persisted layout exists for (project_id, module_id):
    If compatible with the ModuleUISpec:
        Reuse the persisted layout
    Else:
        Rebuild from the ModuleUISpec and record the reason
Else:
    Build from the ModuleUISpec
    ↓
Reconcile the new trees against the current instances
    ↓
LayoutBuilder builds the trees
    ↓
LayoutRenderer renders the trees
    ↓
Workspace repositions Overlays
```

Precedence:

First activation of the Module in the project: materialize the layout declared in the ModuleUISpec.

Subsequent activations: restore the personalized layout for that Module, if it exists and is compatible.

Missing or incompatible layout: rebuild from the ModuleUISpec.

Invalid ModuleUISpec: report a clear error. Do not use the system fallback to hide configuration errors.

### 6.5 Area and Editor reconciliation
Area identity is stable within a layout context (project_id + module_id). It does not imply that area.central is the same instance across all Modules.

Reconciliation rules:

area_id identifies an Area within the current layout context.

If the area_id remains, the Workspace reuses the container and reconciles its internal Editor Layout Tree.

If the internal tree changes:

Editors already present and compatible are preserved.

Editors no longer needed are removed (not destroyed immediately) and follow the removal lifecycle.

New Editors are instantiated and inserted.

If an Area no longer exists in the new composition, it is removed from the layout and follows the disposal lifecycle.

Compatibility is defined per Editor type, not by tree shape. Two different trees may share the same Editor instances.

This avoids destroying an entire Area just because its internal composition changed.

## 7. Overlays
### 7.1 Concept
An Overlay is a floating layer that the Workspace positions over a Region of Areas.

The Overlay:

Does not interfere with the layout — it floats over the Areas.

Is not an Area. It is not hosted by any Area.

Is managed by the Workspace (via OverlayManager).

The Top Bar is not an Overlay. It is a structural component of the Workspace. Overlays are floating elements with their own positioning and hit-testing contracts. The distinction is architectural, not visual.

### 7.2 Regions
A Region is a geometric area derived from the Area Layout Tree that groups related Areas.

A Region is not identified by coordinates alone. Region discovery must consistently determine which Areas compose a region and which bounds delimit it.

Discovery rules:

Placement provides the initial association between Areas and Regions.

Placement is preserved as semantic metadata after construction.

If the user rearranges the tree, the association is revalidated against the current structure.

Placement.CENTRAL may apply to multiple Central Areas in a complex composition.
```
  python
  @dataclass
  class Region:
      id: str                # "central", "left", "right", "bottom"
      bounds: Rect           # rectangle that contains the Region
      areas: list[Area]      # Areas that belong to the Region
      semantic_role: Placement | None
```
Why geometric, with structural support:

Allows arbitrary splits.

Allows multiple Areas per region.

Allows future floating areas.

Avoids fragile inferences based on pixel coordinates.

### 7.3 Overlay geometry and interaction
Each Overlay defines:

Target Region — the Region it floats over.

Anchoring rules — how it aligns within the Region (top, bottom, left, right, margins).

Resize behavior — how it adapts when the Region changes.

Focus behavior — whether it can receive focus.

Hit testing — how to distinguish interaction with the Overlay from interaction with content underneath.

Positioning limits — minimum and maximum sizes, allowed regions.

These contracts are documented in a dedicated specification.

### 7.4 When the layout changes
Whenever the layout changes (new Module, split, window resize, visibility of lateral Areas), the Workspace:

Recomputes the bounds of each Region.

Repositions the Overlays.

The Overlay is reactive.

### 7.5 Other Overlays
The Top Bar is a structural component, not an Overlay.

Future Overlays may include:

Floating tool palettes.

Context menus anchored to selection.

Temporary informational panels.

### 7.6 Persistence
The presence and configuration of Overlays are persisted in OverlayState, alongside the layout trees, inside the project file.
